Checked elaborators across this repository end in a defensive `incoherentCore` branch that re-checks a fact the surrounding certificate chain is supposed to guarantee — 66 such sites across 22 modules at the time of writing. [`PROTOCOL.md`](PROTOCOL.md) already treats the result as an internal failure that "cannot masquerade as an ordinary unsupported request," so the impossibility was being asserted at the public boundary while nothing established it. Proving one such branch dead is cheap; the durable lesson is what made it cheap.

Two structural moves did the work, and both are reusable. First, **let the caller own the composition it depends on**: while base resolution returned the assembled group path, the elaborator needed a separate equation relating that path to the base plus authored segments, and that equation had to be proved through a `do`-block of guards. Returning only the base and composing the path at the use site makes the same equation syntactically true, and the obligation disappears instead of being discharged. Second, **give a sub-step its own closed error type**: forwarding one wide error union through a narrow step forces a proof that the step cannot produce the wider constructors, whereas a two-constructor type makes them unrepresentable. Neither move changes behavior, and the second is the same instinct that already keeps `ValueListKind` and the closed temporal-unit enum honest.

Two smaller mechanics are worth reusing. Extracting a monadic tail into a helper over its already-computed input keeps the star-position obligation stated over exactly the axes that were scanned, which is what makes the bound provable at all; and stating such branch-liveness claims as `≠` over the public entry point keeps them auditable in the trusted registry without exposing private internals. A dead-branch theorem must be paired with executable witnesses that the family's real rejections are still reachable, or it reads as a claim that lowering always succeeds — which is false and would be a much stronger statement than the proof supports.

## LF71 — an unreachable capability is outside the language, and "rejected for dialect X" is not evidence that dialect Y can witness it

> Date: 2026-07-25. Sections: §8. Basis: this project's resolution of [`EXP-2026-07-25-02`](archived/A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER-THROUGH-2026-08-28.md#exp-2026-07-25-02--the-table-enumeration-region-has-no-normative-witness) against pinned kernel 30.8.1 and 31.1.0; no external observation is involved, because the finding is that none is possible.

Table-backed Enumerations looked for most of a session like the clearest structural hole in the eventual-conformance goal. The code generator rejects them for the Groovy and JavaScript targets by declared, diagnosed policy, which made them Java-only, which made the normative Groovy-dynamic anchor unable to witness them, which appeared to force a choice between promoting co-evidence to sole evidence and conceding an uncalibratable region. Both arms were wrong, because the reasoning never checked whether *any* dialect could witness. It cannot: the table accessor is abstract on the tool-internal type, its sole implementor hardcodes an empty optional, and the public model API has no table concept at all. The policy restriction is real and can never fire.

Three transferable conclusions.

**A capability can be unreachable from every in-repo model source while its consumer machinery stays fully maintained.** Checks, templates, runtime clauses, and diagnostics all exist and are kept current; none is reachable. So the presence of maintained consumer machinery is not evidence of reachability, and *"the checker rejects it for dialect X"* is not evidence that dialect Y can witness it — that inference silently assumes a producer.

**Unreachable is a different status from unmodelled, and only one of them is a coverage debt.** An unreachable capability is outside the observable language rather than missing from the account, so it must not be recorded as a conceded hole in a completeness goal. Recording it as one would make the goal look weaker than it is and would attract probe effort that cannot produce an observation.

**The defect shape reached the ruling, not just the findings.** The resolution's own "no evaluation-semantics delta" conclusion was a locally correct reading of two domain-selector methods, generalized past a branch that reaches a third: dependent columns drive a runtime cascading domain filtered by sibling field values, with omission polarity for a not-given subject and value polarity otherwise. A mandated gate check before editing caught it and narrowed the reasoning. This is the fourth recorded instance of *a locally correct statement read outside the branch that reaches it* ([`SOURCES.md`](SOURCES.md#engine-routing-rule--pick-the-layer-by-the-question-not-by-habit) names the shape), and the first inside a conclusion rather than a source claim — which is the argument for gating a resolution on one adversarial check even when the reasoning looks closed.

## LF72 — demoting a route detail does not make it removable; an eager decomposition can make the guard carry the contract

> Date: 2026-07-25. Sections: §8. Basis: accepted [`SPEC-2026-07-25-01`](archived/A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER-THROUGH-2026-08-28.md#spec-2026-07-25-01--the-notall-fields-presence-prepass-is-a-size-selected-route-detail-not-the-observable-contract) and reviewed a12-dmkits revision `dd02b033`, read against this repository's `ValueListQuantifier.evalOrdered` and the peer interpreter's `notAllRes`.

The `NotAll` fields-presence prepass was correctly demoted from the observable contract to one of two size-selected emission routes: the kernel emits an un-prepassed program above its optimization thresholds, and the two agree because the membership test counts only given cells. Both projects landed that correction. The trap is what the correction invites next — the local specification went on to advise modelling "the unconditional form," and that advice was wrong.

**The guard is load-bearing whenever values-side poison is applied eagerly.** `evalOrdered .notAll` reaches `collectPoisoningValueListMembers` only after the presence guard passes. Remove the guard and a poisoning values member decides UNKNOWN before any fields witness exists, so a document whose fields side has no present cell returns `unknown` where the contract requires `notFired`. The kernel's un-prepassed route must therefore apply poison **at fire time**, where no fire means no escalation — which is what the independently verified route equivalence entails, not a separate reading of that method's body. Eager-classification and fire-time-poison decompositions are not interchangeable, and only the second may consume the values side unconditionally.

This is not a peculiarity of one encoding: the peer interpreter's `notAllRes` guards identically and then does `vlqValuesOf(...) ?: return UNKNOWN`, so both independent clean-room implementations depend on the guard. A shared correction that is true about the engine can still be unsafe as implementation guidance, which is the reusable point — **a claim about what the engine does not observe is not a claim about what an implementation may omit.** When demoting a mechanism from the contract, check every consumer whose result domain is wider than the observable one before describing it as optional.

The prior instances of the session's named defect shape were statements read outside the branch that reaches them. This one is its dual: a statement read outside the *decomposition* that sustains it. Guard against it the same way — name the layer, and re-derive the claim at the consumer rather than inheriting it.

## LF73 — a family can carry two structurally similar short-circuits with opposite status, and only proof tells them apart

> Date: 2026-07-25. Sections: §8. Basis: experiment E1 of the archived [`SEMANTIC-CORE-IL-PROPOSAL.md`](archived/SEMANTIC-CORE-IL-PROPOSAL.md), against this repository's own value-list sources; no external observation is involved.

The value-list quantifier family has two guards that look alike in source and in prose. `NotAll` checks for a present fields cell before consuming values; `AtLeastOne` checks for an empty member set before scanning fields. Both read as ordinary optimizations. They are not the same kind of thing.

`AtLeastOne`'s guard is **redundant**: with no members the ordered scan already exhausts to non-firing, so removing it changes nothing, which `scanAtLeastOne_nil_members` proves. `NotAll`'s guard is **load-bearing** for any eagerly classifying encoding, because removing it lets a poisoning member decide UNKNOWN before a witness exists — [`LF72`](#lf72--demoting-a-route-detail-does-not-make-it-removable-an-eager-decomposition-can-make-the-guard-carry-the-contract). One is safe to drop and one silently changes a result, and nothing in their shape or wording distinguishes them.

The reusable lesson is about method rather than about value lists. **A short-circuit's status is a theorem, not a reading.** Source inspection tells you a guard exists and prose tells you someone believed it was an optimization; only an equivalence proof over the unguarded form tells you which. So when demoting a guard from a contract — the move `SPEC-2026-07-25-01` made correctly at the engine level — prove the unguarded form equivalent in the actual encoding before describing it as removable. **Amended 2026-07-25, when the core-IL experiment was closed.** The finding was first proved through that experiment's helpers; both it and the retained theorem now live at the family, which is where the fact was always about. E1 also established a second, genuinely new result worth keeping here rather than in source: **the two firing scans instantiate one traversal.** `scanValueListAtLeastOneFields` and `scanValueListNotAllFields` share the same recursion and the same `Verdict` result domain, with membership direction as the only semantic parameter — they are not two algorithms but one mechanism under opposite predicates, which is why their guards can differ in status while their scans do not differ in shape. The parameterized helper that demonstrated this was **retired**, because keeping it beside both established scans was net duplication with no independent consumer. The relationship is durable knowledge; a second implementation of it was not.

## LF74 — an environment that is a union of per-family parameters is not an abstraction, and its theorem statements will say so

> Date: 2026-07-25. Sections: §6 and §8. Basis: experiments E1/E2 and the follow-up unit of the archived [`SEMANTIC-CORE-IL-PROPOSAL.md`](archived/SEMANTIC-CORE-IL-PROPOSAL.md), against this repository's own sources.

A shared evaluator was given its first family's state as two positional arguments. Adding a second family added a third argument, because the new family read classified operands rather than operand streams. Each addition type-checked and every proof stayed green, so nothing failed loudly — but the evaluator had become a union of per-family environments rather than an abstraction over them, and a third family would have added a fourth argument.

**The symptom to watch for is in the theorem statements, not in the code.** The numeric preservation theorems were forced to bind `fields` and `values` that they never used, purely because the evaluator's signature demanded them. A binder a theorem must carry but never mentions is the visible form of the defect: it says the interface is shaped by a family other than the one being stated.

The fix was one addressed slot space read by one node, with per-family slot layouts named as constructors rather than left as bare indices. Two constructs collapsed into one, so the core lost a construct while gaining a family, the dead binders disappeared, and every preservation theorem in both families took the same shape. The reusable test is therefore cheap: **after adding a family to a shared mechanism, check whether any existing statement had to grow a binder it does not use.** If so, the mechanism has accumulated a union and should be abstracted before the next family, because each one added first widens what has to be unpicked.

The complementary caution is that fixing the environment does not automatically fix the *result* domain, which can accumulate the same way. Whether it does is bounded by result domains rather than by families, and only a third family can establish that.

**Amended 2026-07-25, when the experiment was closed and this "fix" was rejected.** The detection half of this finding stands and is the part worth keeping: *a binder a theorem must carry but never mentions is the visible symptom of an accumulated union*, and it is a cheap, reliable signal. **The remedy half does not.** An external review established that the addressed slot space did not abstract over the per-family union — it relocated it into an untyped `List CoreValue` indexed by a bare `Nat`, so every family still contributed its own layout and builder while `read` could return any constructor. What actually improved was the *signature*; the semantic ABI was left open, and the disappearing binders measured the former rather than the latter.

So the sharper lesson is a warning about this finding's own evidence: **dead binders vanishing proves the signature stopped growing, not that the union was eliminated.** A real fix would have given reads a type or schema — here, cell observations against a document with structural failure kept distinct from semantic UNKNOWN, which [`ARCHITECTURE.md`](ARCHITECTURE.md) already requires of an extrinsic AST via an explicit `WellFormed` boundary that was never written. When a symptom disappears, check whether the mechanism did too; see [`LF76`](#lf76--success-criteria-written-by-the-agent-that-will-be-graded-against-them-converge-on-that-agents-framing).

## LF75 — writing a research guard and applying it are different acts; the same commit can do the first and fail the second

> Date: 2026-07-25. Sections: §5 and §7. Basis: reviewed a12-dmkits revisions `97e35531` and `724926a5`, against this repository's own `spec/` and commit `100de09`.

The 2026-07-25 exchange with a12-dmkits produced a guard for [`SOURCES.md`](SOURCES.md#engine-routing-rule--pick-the-layer-by-the-question-not-by-habit): *a peer's reachability claim is a hypothesis until measured, and "unrepresentable" is the most tempting unmeasured one.* It was written after two inferences had been adopted here as measured fact and then withdrawn upstream.

**The commit that added that paragraph adopted a third one in the same diff.** The peer's intermediate draft argued the parse-time lift's witness needs 17 fractional digits against a field scale cap of 14, so no legal model can express it; `spec/07` was rewritten to say the classifier is exact-decimal for every authorable input. The peer retracted it two revisions later: the literal gate is `MVK_INVALID_COMPARE_DEC_PLACES`, the kernel's one suppressible code, so the witness is authorable with the documented directive.

Three properties make this worth keeping rather than filing as another instance of the shape named in `SOURCES.md`:

- **The defeater was in this project's own canonical prose**, two files from the edit. [`04-numbers-and-decimals.md`](../spec/04-numbers-and-decimals.md) states that the suppression "allows an `==`/`!=` across differing scales" and is the *only* suppressible code; [`12-concrete-syntax.md`](../spec/12-concrete-syntax.md) carries its grammar. Nothing had to be measured, requested, or read from a sibling — only recalled. The earlier instances of this shape at least required evidence the repository did not hold.
- **The guard's own text names the failure it then permitted.** Proximity to a rule is not application of it, and a rule written in the same session is if anything *less* likely to be applied, because writing it feels like discharging it.
- **The Lean was right the whole time.** `roundToBinary64` already modelled the parse step, so the executable theory would have classified the witness correctly on the day the prose declared it unreachable. Every defect in this exchange was in prose *about* the formalisation.

The transferable rule is narrow and mechanical, and it is now in `SOURCES.md`: **before consuming an unreachability claim, grep this repository's `spec/` for the gate it names.** An unreachability argument is a conjunction, so it falls to a single defeater; the cheapest place to look for one is the prose this project already owns. A conformance case is the durable form of that check — the case added here carries the authorable pair and would have failed against a model that treated the classifier as exact-decimal, which the retracted clause invited a reimplementer to build.

## LF76 — success criteria written by the agent that will be graded against them converge on that agent's framing

> Date: 2026-07-25. Sections: cross-cutting. Basis: the closed core-IL experiment (archived [`SEMANTIC-CORE-IL-PROPOSAL.md`](archived/SEMANTIC-CORE-IL-PROPOSAL.md) §10) and an external review of it, against this repository's own sources.

The core-IL proposal pre-registered four success criteria before the work, which is the right discipline and is why [`LF1`](#lf1--differential-evidence-and-proofs-answer-different-questions)-style pre-registration is used at all. All four passed. The experiment was nonetheless at a materially different boundary from the one the proposal claimed, contained a docstring citing a theorem that was never written, and carried three helpers that re-expressed existing family algorithms.

**The criteria were the defect.** They measured constructor count, theorem greenness, universality, and transport-by-rewriting. None asked the questions that mattered: does the core sit at the boundary the proposal promised; does a helper duplicate an existing owner; does a cited theorem exist. Criteria written by the agent that will grade against them inherit that agent's framing, so passing them confirms the framing rather than testing it.

What makes this worth recording rather than filing as ordinary error is that **every finding was checkable against text in the same repository, usually the same file**:

- `ValueList.lean`'s own module docstring says the capsule "starts after both operands have been expanded per cell and filtered", and `ofNumericPair`'s says "already-classified" — while the proposal promised lowering "from each checked family".
- `runScanUntilMatch_eq_scanNo`, since removed, was filed as evidence of adequacy. Read once more it says an existing function was re-implemented.
- "Eight of ten" sat directly above a table with six `yes` rows.

So this is the same shape as [`LF75`](#lf75--writing-a-research-guard-and-applying-it-are-different-acts-the-same-commit-can-do-the-first-and-fail-the-second) and the "read the guard you already have on screen" rule in [`SOURCES.md`](SOURCES.md#engine-routing-rule--pick-the-layer-by-the-question-not-by-habit) — unread evidence, not missing evidence — but reached through the *assessment*, which is the layer meant to catch the others.

Three transferable checks, ordered by cost:

1. **Count anything numeric in prose against the thing it summarizes.** Seconds, and it caught nothing here because it was never run.
2. **For every `X_eq_Y` theorem, ask whether `Y` pre-existed, and if so why `X` exists.** Agreement is not redundancy — every legitimate lowering has an equivalence theorem — but agreement *plus* an identical boundary, mirrored recursion, no independent consumer, and no unique invariant is. That question alone retired three of four helpers.
3. **Diff the implementation's actual boundary against the boundary the owning document claims**, quoting both. The two sentences convicting this experiment were in files it had just edited.

The mechanizable part is now a gate rather than a resolution: `check-lean-trust.sh` rejects a backticked identifier that resolves to no declaration, which is exactly the `lowerValueListQuantifier_wellFormed` defect. That gate covers Lean sources only, and the obvious extension to `docs/` is **not** worth building: the scan was run once by hand over every live document and returned three names, all of them historical references inside findings that exist to record why those declarations went away. A gate would reject exactly the citations a post-mortem needs. The rest is not mechanizable, which is the argument for the cold-review triggers in [`TESTING.md`](TESTING.md#trigger-based-cold-review) applying to a *proposal's own criteria* and not only to its code — an author-run assessment is a consistency check, not an adversarial one.

## LF77 — a dependency graph can order every computation without deciding which dependency failures propagate

> Date: 2026-07-26. Sections: §11. Basis: pinned kernel 30.8.1 `CalculationDependencies`, `CodeGenCalcBuilder`, generated Java `CodeGenCalc`/`CodeGenCalculationAlternative`, `CompositeCondition`, and `CalculationCache` at revision `cb66e51fa7ab90b650698f861bf670754e2e1e66`; the existing a12-dmkits `ComputePoisonReadDiffTest` tri-check originating at revision `e70b9af34fb144b2f8af9241867730f25aea5a62`; and the canonical reached-read account in [`spec/09-computations.md`](../spec/09-computations.md).

The V2 service Javadoc says a computation “relying directly or indirectly” on an invalid field is skipped. Read as a graph rule, that implies an invalid producer should pre-skip every syntactic dependent even when the dependent's earlier branch decides before the producer reference. That account conflicts with the established poison-on-read behavior, but only if the dependency graph is assumed to carry failure propagation as well as order.

It does not. The tool layer collects every authored field reference and topologically sorts calculated fields from that conservative graph. The generated Java runtime then calls every calculated-field method in the resulting order. Invalidity propagation lives inside those methods: `CalculationCache` throws only when an invalid field's value or presence is actually accessed, and each generated alternative catches that exception and marks its own target invalid. Generated composite conditions use native `&&`/`||`, so a decisive earlier branch can prevent the access. The graph makes the producer final before a possible read; it does not make that read happen.

The durable representation consequence is that **schedule dependency and dynamic failure dependency are different relations even when they originate from the same authored reference**. A checked whole-model computation plan may conservatively retain the static edge for cycle checking and order, but its operational semantics must propagate poison through reached reads. Precomputing a transitive poisoned set from the graph is not an optimization of that semantics; it changes it. Conversely, a read-driven overlay does not remove the need for the static graph, because a potential read still constrains order even when this document's execution short-circuits it.

This closes only that ambiguity. Same-target nonrepeatable String selection is now closed separately by LF22 and accepted `SPEC-2026-07-26-03`; runtime-instance construction, cross-family result ownership, and the independently observed coarse parallel-index clearing rule remain SG4 concerns. No new kernel probe was required: the generated-program route fixes the combined graph/read mechanism, and the maintained dual-strategy differential already fixes each observable short-circuit and poison component.

## LF78 — one checked index column shares duplicate exclusion without sharing selection policy

> Date: 2026-07-26, corrected 2026-08-09. Sections: §9 and §10. Basis: pinned kernel 30.8.1 index-cache sources at revision `cb66e51fa7ab90b650698f861bf670754e2e1e66`; maintained a12-dmkits `ParallelJoinDuplicateKeyDiffTest` and semantic-index differentials at clean revision `752054077be62be441b119af14efc97c6baf13d9`; red/green checked-column separators; and trusted common-admission, semantic-index, parallel-index, unmatched-side, and well-formedness laws.

“The index column is invalid” is not one executable row-selection policy, but duplicate admission is shared. Semantic-index validation excludes every duplicate-key participant from its lookup entries, and parallel iteration likewise never selects a duplicated entry. If the clean far side supplies the same key, the duplicated side is unmatched and its column marker makes that side UNKNOWN. If both sides duplicate the key, neither contributes it to the union and no all-unmatched phantom row is created.

The shared carrier still retains three independent facts: every ordered normalized-key occurrence with its physical `Env`, duplicate-key membership, and one column-unavailability cause. The common `admitsSelectableKey` relation consumes only duplicate membership. Semantic index then builds its clean target map, while parallel iteration independently builds a sorted outer join and applies its missing-side observation. One `poisoned` or `unknown` constructor cannot reconstruct those distinct post-admission policies.

The earlier account that made duplicate keys parallel-addressable and selected the last immutable-document occurrence was wrong. It promoted a source-level cache insertion detail into a row-selection rule even though the maintained tri-engine differential directly shows that duplicated values are never selectable. Lean now preserves occurrences for the checked carrier while excluding them before both key-union construction and side lookup.

The kernel's unmatched pointer sentinel is also not a physical address. Lean represents that state as `Option Env`; matched environments remain complete positive named bindings, while `none` selects the missing-side observation policy. This preserves the existing invariant that row zero or any other nonphysical coordinate is structural failure rather than semantic UNKNOWN.

The first parallel projection deliberately stops at exact-text direct siblings. Kernel Number identity is numeric, but parallel union order is the lexical order of the declaration-rendered normalized key. Lean has normalized `Rat` identity but not that declaration/locale rendering boundary, so accepting Number parallel joins now would guess an observable order. Semantic index can already consume the Number column because lookup needs identity, not rendered sort order.

## LF79 — resolving a host-SPI name during elaboration inverts a boundary the model check never had

> Date: 2026-08-05. Sections: §5 and §14. Basis: pinned kernel 30.8.1 `CheckPredefinedTypeValidityConditionImpl` at revision `cb66e51fa7ab90b650698f861bf670754e2e1e66`; the accepted [`EXP-2026-08-04-01`](archived/A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER-THROUGH-2026-08-28.md#exp-2026-08-04-01--an-unregistered-predefined-type-may-raise-rather-than-fire-both-polarities) handback at a12-dmkits `dd0bcadf`; and the red/green registration × fill × polarity matrix in [`Conformance/CustomFieldValidity.lean`](../A12Kernel/Conformance/CustomFieldValidity.lean).

A checked layer naturally resolves a name to the thing it names, so `CheckedCustomFieldValidity` stored a `RegisteredCustomFieldValidator` and elaboration rejected any name the injected `World` did not supply. That reads like ordinary fail-closed discipline. It is not: the kernel's model check for these predicates verifies the operand and a nonempty type name and **never consults the registry**, because the predefined types are a host client SPI that exists only at runtime. The checked layer had therefore rejected a legal model, and no conformance case could see it, because every case supplied the validator it was asking about.

The durable rule is that a **partial** host registry must be represented by carrying the *resolution outcome* into the certificate, not by making resolution a precondition of building one. `validator? : Option RegisteredCustomFieldValidator` keeps elaboration registry-blind beyond recording what the world answered, which is why one law (`elaborateCustomFieldValidity_admits`) covers the resolved and unsupplied certificates as two rewrites instead of two theorems with opposite verdicts.

The proof consequence is sharper than the representation one. The attractive law here — `Invalid ↔ ¬Valid` — is true only where a validator exists, and the unregistered arm makes **both** polarities fire on one value. A capsule that resolves at elaboration time can state that law unconditionally and stay green forever, because its own certificate makes the counterexample unrepresentable. `customFieldValidity_complement_needs_validator` proves the unrestricted generalization false, so the hypothesis cannot be quietly dropped by a later refactor. **An elaboration-time precondition that narrows the model also narrows what a law can be falsified by; prefer a total certificate with an explicit hypothesis over a certificate that cannot express the case.**

Two limits. The two kernel states in which no usable validator exists — no factory supplied at all, and a factory present that returns nothing for the name — are measured indistinguishable, so collapsing them into one `none` is accurate rather than lossy. And the double firing's cause is unresolved on both sides; this capsule encodes the measured observable only, and [`SG7`](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md#sg7--string-pattern-and-custom-field-completion) carries the open mechanism plus the same unresolved partiality question on the declared-custom-field path.

## LF80 — a separator that both accounts satisfy is not a separator, and a boolean lock hides which one you have

> Date: 2026-08-05. Sections: §7 and §10. Basis: reviewed a12-dmkits `ddaf2e13`, whose measurement disproved this project's suppression account for `FieldValuesNotUnique` and the peer's identically-worded one; the retained matrix in [`Conformance/NumberValuesNotUnique.lean`](../A12Kernel/Conformance/NumberValuesNotUnique.lean); and [`SOURCES.md`](SOURCES.md) for the exact rows.

Two clean-room implementations independently published "a formally-invalid operand suppresses this operator", both retained a case for it, and both were wrong. The kernel *skips* an invalid cell exactly like an empty one, so a duplicate on either side of it still fires. Neither implementation's case caught it, and the reason is structural rather than careless: the case behind the claim had **one** invalid operand and no duplicate. Under suppression the answer is silence; under skipping the invalid value simply leaves the comparison and the answer is still silence. The observation was identical for both accounts, so retaining it proved nothing about which one the engine implements.

What separates them is a case where the two accounts *disagree*: an invalid cell **beside a duplicate**. Suppression predicts silence, skipping predicts a firing. Its partner is two **equal** invalid values, where skipping predicts silence because neither value enters the comparison — that pair pins the mechanism from both sides, and neither case existed anywhere until the peer built it.

The durable rule is a test on retained cases, not on code: **for each retained case, name the rival account it kills.** If the nearest rival predicts the same observation, the case is documentation, not evidence, and the family is unlocked no matter how green it is. This is sharper than "add a negative control", because the case here *was* a negative control — a real observation of real silence. Its defect was that silence is what both accounts predict.

A second-order consequence is about what a lock asserts. Both implementations asserted a boolean — that no message appeared. A lock that compares only firing cannot distinguish "did not fire because suppressed" from "did not fire because nothing duplicated", and it will keep passing after the semantics underneath it changes. Where two accounts differ in *why* an outcome occurs rather than in the outcome itself, the retained case has to reach for the discriminating shape rather than a richer assertion on the same shape.

Cost of the miss, recorded because it argues the rule's value: the peer's engine failed to fire rules the kernel fires, which is the worst direction for a validation engine, and it survived every green gate on both sides for as long as the operator had been modelled.

## LF81 — correcting a clause does not correct the operator while a shared upstream gate still pre-empts it

> Date: 2026-08-05. Sections: §7 and §10. Basis: this repository's own commits `c70eaea` (the incomplete correction) and its successor; the checked-document cases in [`Conformance/NumberValuesNotUnique.lean`](../A12Kernel/Conformance/NumberValuesNotUnique.lean); `collectTaggedValueListCells` in [`Semantics/ValueList.lean`](../A12Kernel/Semantics/ValueList.lean).

The `FieldValuesNotUnique` skip correction from [`LF80`](#lf80--a-separator-that-both-accounts-satisfy-is-not-a-separator-and-a-boolean-lock-hides-which-one-you-have) landed in the evaluator, with a green matrix asserting that a duplicate beside an unavailable cell fires. The operator still answered UNKNOWN, because both checked consumers reached that evaluator through `scanResolvedValueListOperands`, whose contract is "stop at the first unavailable resolved cell". The corrected clause was never reached on the exact input class the correction was about. Every retained case addressed the pure evaluator directly, so no case could observe the pre-empting gate one layer up.

Two distinct rules follow, and the second is the load-bearing one.

**Anchor a corrected case at the boundary a real rule enters, not at the clause you edited.** A pure-clause case proves the clause; it says nothing about reachability. Where a correction changes behaviour on an input class, at least one retained case must run the consumer that a rule actually invokes, because only that path contains the gates. Here the checked-document route needed a fixture with a `.rejected` cell; the pure case needed one constructor.

**A shared helper's contract can carry the very assumption under correction.** `scanResolvedValueListOperands` earns its availability gate honestly: the aggregate, distinct-count, and value-count families route through the kernel's shared all-cells combiner, which returns its non-relevant value at the first unavailable cell. Reuse propagated that gate to the one operator in the family whose kernel counterpart owns its own loop and merely skips. The fix is a sibling collector, `collectTaggedValueListCells`, that resolves in authored order and gates nothing, with each helper's docstring naming the kernel mechanism it belongs to. Splitting the helper is what makes the two accounts visible at the call site; a boolean parameter on one helper would have hidden the same divergence behind a flag.

The generalizable check, cheap enough to run on every correction: **after fixing a clause, grep its consumers and ask which one a rule reaches, then confirm the new input class survives that path.** A correction to a shared mechanism is unfinished while any consumer still short-circuits ahead of it, and the surviving gate will not show up in a green build.

## LF82 — an admission gate can already supply the identity a new atom was going to invent

> Date: 2026-08-05. Sections: §7 and §9. Basis: the corrected temporal entry packet in [`SEMANTICS-GAPS.md`](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md#sg7--string-pattern-and-custom-field-completion); reviewed a12-dmkits `ddaf2e13`; kernel `RuntimeController.feldWerteNichtEindeutig` reading `feldWertIntern` against `anzahlVerschiedenerWerteDatum` reading `feldWertAlsDatum`; [`Elaboration/TemporalValuesNotUnique.lean`](../A12Kernel/Elaboration/TemporalValuesNotUnique.lean).

The temporal `FieldValuesNotUnique` arm was planned as an extension of the shared `ValueListKind` with a new atom carrying decoded temporal identity, and the plan recorded that it wanted a fresh working context because it would touch a type six modules depend on. It needed neither. The operator's admission gate requires **one identical declared format string** across every operand, which makes the operand list textually homogeneous; the kernel reads `feldWertIntern` — the stored text — for this operator; so the compared value *is* the stored text, which the canonical token atom already holds exactly. The arm closed as a new certificate and one projection function with the shared type untouched.

The generalizable move is to read the admission gate before designing the value domain. A gate that constrains declarations can collapse a heterogeneous-looking value domain into an existing one, and the collapse is exact rather than lossy — no normal form is invented, because the compared bytes are the ones the engine compares. The packet's two rejected alternatives were rejected for the right reasons and both would have been real costs: *rendering* a decoded value into text invents a normal form the engine lacks, and reusing the Number atom imports the scale-19 comparison boundary. What the packet missed is that *retaining* the stored text is neither of those.

The second lesson is sharper and is why this is a finding rather than a note. **Two operators over the same checked entity lists can read different value identities**, and nothing in their shared plumbing signals it. `FieldValuesNotUnique` reads stored text; `NumberOfDifferentValues` reads the decoded date over the same lists. Had the temporal atom been shared between them on the strength of that shared plumbing, one of the two would have been wrong, and the retained cases for each would still have passed — a duplicate-detection case and a distinct-count case rarely disagree on a well-formed list. Before reusing a value domain across neighbouring operators, name the engine accessor each one calls; family resemblance is not evidence, which is the same rule the skip-versus-suppress split established from the other direction ([`LF80`](#lf80--a-separator-that-both-accounts-satisfy-is-not-a-separator-and-a-boolean-lock-hides-which-one-you-have)).

The limit is explicit and lives with the clause: stored-text and decoded identity agree only if one declared format admits one text per value. That is an assumption, not an observation, and collapsing the domain is what makes the assumption *visible* — it is one sentence in one projection function with a named witness class, where a decoded atom would have buried the same question inside a decoder. Prefer the representation that localizes an unmeasured claim to the representation that distributes it.

## LF83 — modelling only *that* a model is refused loses an observable, and the loss hides a real divergence

> Date: 2026-08-05. Sections: §9 and §10. Basis: measured coverage of the static-legality axis (34 of roughly 57 in-scope `MVK_` codes named in `spec/`, six referenced in Lean before this unit); the gate-order rows measured at a12-dmkits `ddaf2e13`; [`Elaboration/StaticDiagnostic.lean`](../A12Kernel/Elaboration/StaticDiagnostic.lean) and the retained cases in [`Conformance/TemporalValuesNotUnique.lean`](../A12Kernel/Conformance/TemporalValuesNotUnique.lean).

This project's elaborators refused illegal models correctly and carried 118 richly typed error inductives, none of which said **which** Kernel diagnostic the model would actually draw. That felt like an error-message convenience left for later. It is not: which class the Kernel reports is observable, two illegal models that both fail authoring are still distinguishable if they report different classes, and three consumer profiles act on the difference — an importer deciding repairability, a refactoring tool preserving the class its transformation triggers, an Explain surface naming the failure.

The concrete cost was a divergence that no existing gate could see. The Kernel runs two admission gates and the kind gate pre-empts the mixing gate, which was already in `spec/`. What was not stated is the gate's **scope**: each gate applies to the whole operand list. This project refused at the first offending operand in authored order, so a list of `Date, String, Boolean` reported the mixing class where the Kernel reports the kind class. Every case still passed, because every case asserted only that the list was refused. The account was right about refusal and wrong about identity, and only representing identity could expose that.

Two design rules earned their place here.

**Represent an unestablished class as `none`, never as the nearest plausible name.** The projection is `Error → Option KernelStaticDiagnostic`, and five of the temporal overload's refusals legitimately return `none`: an insufficiency of this project's own, a duplicated operand with no measured class, a filter failure belonging elsewhere. Mapping those to a plausible-looking `MVK_` name would have asserted an unmeasured correspondence in the one place a consumer is most likely to trust it. The `Option` also makes coverage countable, which is what turned this axis from an impression into a measurement.

**Grow the vocabulary one family at a time, admitting only classes that family's measured rows establish.** The alternative — enumerating all 280 Kernel constants, or even the roughly 57 in scope, and filling in mappings by reading — would produce exactly the kind of confident-looking unmeasured claim that costs a reconciliation round. CONFIRM was the live example when this finding was written: it sat beside BOOLEAN while the peer's table covered BOOLEAN only, so the temporal consumer kept it `unestablished`. A later clean 24-case matrix at a12-dmkits `cd41ea94` established CONFIRM as the same outright kind refusal and justified promoting it to `refusedByKind`; the evidence arrived before the stronger classification did.

The limit worth stating: a projection is only as good as the rows behind it, and one generalization here was explicitly *not* measured when this finding was written. The pre-emption was observed for one operand order; whole-list scope is the source-shaped reading of a gate over a list, and [`SPEC-2026-08-05-04`](archived/A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER-THROUGH-2026-08-28.md#spec-2026-08-05-04--a-field-list-admission-gate-applies-to-the-whole-operand-list-so-authored-position-does-not-select-the-diagnostic-class) asked for the reordered pair before either project relied on it. That pair is now measured at a12-dmkits `5f1c08dc` for both the kind and format gates, with the pre-empted code asserted absent, and the entry is accepted.

## LF84 — a handback's claims enter `spec/` through an unguarded door, and the discharge table was written only for claims going out

> Date: 2026-08-06. Sections: §3 and §12. Basis: a12-dmkits `4ae043b9` accepted here, then falsified by that project's own cold review at `e01bd0de`; the ledger receipt [`SPEC-2026-08-06-01`](archived/A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER-THROUGH-2026-08-28.md#spec-2026-08-06-01--value-aggregate-suppression-is-an-ordered-first-cell-early-return-not-a-whole-selection-property) and its successor; and this repository's own [`spec/10` §5](../spec/10-validation-and-polarity.md#5-full-vs-partial-validation).

The claim-discharge table is phrased for claims *leaving* this repository — `spec/`, a ledger entry, a docstring, a reply. Marking a ledger entry `accepted` is the other direction, and it is the widest inbound door there is: it writes a peer's account into `spec/` and into an immutable receipt in one act. Nothing gated it. A handback asserted that a positional pair "no whole-selection scan can produce", and that mechanism claim was accepted, recorded as normative, and propagated into the third-state table, a gap entry, a provenance row, and a Lean docstring before the peer's own cold review withdrew it.

**The defeater was local, and cheaper to find than the claim was to accept.** `spec/10` §5 already stated that an all-rows aggregate stays UNKNOWN in partial validation *even when every concrete row is enumerated* — which is a whole-selection survey, in this project's own prose, contradicting the clause being written one file over. [`LF75`](#lf75--writing-a-research-guard-and-applying-it-are-different-acts-the-same-commit-can-do-the-first-and-fail-the-second) already prescribes exactly the search that would have found it. It went unrun because it is phrased for *unreachability* claims, and this one was an *exclusivity* claim: not "no model can express it" but "no other mechanism can produce it". Those are the same logical shape — a negative existential, defeated by one witness — and only the first was on the checklist.

Three transferable rules, cheapest first:

1. **Any negative existential gets the same treatment, whatever its wording.** "Unrepresentable", "no legal model", "cannot be read off", "only X produces this", "no other account explains it" are one class. Grep this repository's own `spec/` for the mechanism the claim excludes before consuming it.
2. **A measurement that both rival accounts predict discharges the outcome and nothing else.** This is [`LF80`](#lf80--a-separator-that-both-accounts-satisfy-is-not-a-separator-and-a-boolean-lock-hides-which-one-you-have) reappearing one level up: there it invalidated a retained *case*, here it invalidated an *acceptance*. Verdict identity under a head/tail swap was offered as proof of an ordered scan and is equally consistent with a survey. When a handback's evidence is an outcome and its claim is a mechanism, the claim is undischarged no matter how green the lock.
3. **Separate the outcome from its locus when recording either.** The suppression outcome was and remains measured; only "at the first such cell" fell. Had the clause stated the total function unconditionally and kept the locus as a separate annotation allowed to be unknown, the retraction would have touched one annotation instead of five surfaces — the same coupling rule the discharge table already states for reachability, applying just as well to mechanism.

The asymmetry is the durable part: this project has a script gating `spec/` against the ledger, a trust script gating proofs, and a capsule-closure assessment gating its own work. The acceptance step has no gate but a reading, and it is the one step where another project's confidence becomes this project's normative text.

**Postscript, same day: the method ran in both directions and found a divergence in this project's code.** a12-dmkits applied the rule above to its own prose and found a clause naming five operators as evaluating only under a wildcard, tagged verified with a cited lock that exercised `Sum` alone. Measured per operator, `SumOfProducts` and `FirstFilledValue` fire where the other four are silent, because each owns its loop and reaches neither the shared combiner nor its pre-loop survey. That over-wide list had reached the peer's interpreter as a real kernel divergence, and it had reached **this project's Lean** the same way: `CheckedNumericProductAggregate.evaluatePartial` gated `SumOfProducts` on all-rows extent coverage inherited from the aggregate family, so it answered non-relevant where the Kernel evaluates. Nothing was wrong with the proofs, and the full build stayed green through the fix, because no law had ever depended on the gate.

So the sharpest form of the check is about a denominator, and it generalizes [`LF75`](#lf75--writing-a-research-guard-and-applying-it-are-different-acts-the-same-commit-can-do-the-first-and-fail-the-second)'s "count anything numeric in prose against the thing it summarizes" from numbers to **lists**: **when a clause names a set of operators and cites a lock, count the set against the cases the lock actually exercises.** A list of five backed by one measured member is four unmeasured claims wearing a verified tag, and the tag is what stops anyone re-checking them. The failure mode is specific to shared-mechanism prose: the moment a clause says "these operators behave alike", the cheap thing to write is the list and the expensive thing is the per-member measurement, so the list is what gets written.

## LF85 — a claim already sitting in this project's own documents is not thereby discharged, and restating it is a fresh claim

> Date: 2026-08-06. Sections: §3, §9, and §12. Basis: three same-day defects traced to their sources — `spec/02`'s ordered aggregate claim against this repository's own [`spec/10` §5](../spec/10-validation-and-polarity.md#5-full-vs-partial-validation); [`SG7`](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md#sg7--string-pattern-and-custom-field-completion)'s withdrawn blocker against a12-dmkits `12491e4f`; and [`SPEC-2026-08-05-04`](archived/A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER-THROUGH-2026-08-28.md#spec-2026-08-05-04--a-field-list-admission-gate-applies-to-the-whole-operand-list-so-authored-position-does-not-select-the-diagnostic-class)'s "no probe here reaches the Kernel" against this repository's own [`TESTING.md`](TESTING.md#structured-dmtool-probes-and-feedback).

[`LF84`](#lf84--a-handbacks-claims-enter-spec-through-an-unguarded-door-and-the-discharge-table-was-written-only-for-claims-going-out) closed the inbound door: a peer's claim gets classified and discharged before it becomes normative text here. Three defects landed the same day that the door was not the problem. **Every one came from a sentence this project already owned**, carried into a new place without being rechecked, and in each case the refuting evidence was in a file this repository maintains.

1. `spec/02` stated aggregate suppression as an ordered stop at the first non-relevant cell. `spec/10` §5 already said an all-rows aggregate stays UNKNOWN when every concrete row is enumerated, which is a whole-repetition survey. The two clauses contradicted each other inside this repository for a day.
2. `SG7` recorded a blocker whose entire premise was that two a12-dmkits surfaces disagreed. Both halves were misread. Their skill says "one cross-row aggregate verdict"; their finding measures one message at the first-row anchor and states outright that it is not a declaration-level address. The blocker was withdrawn, not resolved, because there had never been a conflict.
3. `SPEC-2026-08-05-04` justified an upstream request with "no probe here reaches the Kernel". `TESTING.md` states in its own probe section that `rule check` and `model check` call the real kernel consistency oracle and can support a static `KERNEL_CONFIRMED` claim. The entry's question was a static admission question. **It was measurable here in two commands, and it was sent upstream instead.**

The third is the expensive one, and it is expensive in the direction that is hardest to notice: nothing was wrong, a peer answered promptly, and the only cost was a round trip nobody could see was avoidable. The first two produced a false clause and a phantom blocker; this one produced *work*.

**The rule.** A sentence in `spec/`, a gap entry, `PLAN.md`, `SOURCES.md`, `TESTING.md`, or a prior ledger entry carries no more authority than a peer's assertion. It was written by an agent with the same incentives and no better evidence, and its age is not evidence. So restating it in a new place — a clause, a request, a blocker, a justification — is a **fresh claim-leaving act** and takes the discharge its class requires. The specific trap is that quoting yourself feels like citing a source.

Three checks, cheapest first, each of which would have caught one of the three:

1. **Before writing a mechanism or ordering claim into `spec/`, grep `spec/` for the neighbouring clause that would contradict it.** Sibling clauses about the same gate are where the defeater lives, because they were written from a different question.
2. **Before recording a blocker whose premise is that someone else's two artifacts disagree, open both artifacts.** A disagreement is a conjunction of two readings, and a blocker is the most expensive thing to be wrong about, because it stops work rather than producing something checkable.
3. **Before writing "no route here reaches X", grep this project's own process documents for X.** The routing answer lives in `SOURCES.md`'s engine-routing rule and `TESTING.md`'s probe section, and "we cannot measure this" is the single claim most likely to be false and least likely to be challenged, because it closes an inquiry rather than opening one.

The through-line with `LF75` and `LF84` is now explicit and worth stating once: this project keeps writing guards it then fails to consult. `LF75` was writing a guard and not applying it in the same commit. `LF84` was applying the outbound discharge table to nothing inbound. This is failing to consult guards that had been correct and sitting on disk for weeks. The mechanizable fraction is small; what generalizes is that **the cheapest defeater for a claim is almost always inside this repository**, so the first search should be local and only then upstream.

## LF86 — refusing claims nothing, extending by parity claims something, and an owning gap's "do not widen" is not waived by a sibling arm

A capsule implemented a group operand's descendant set as the whole subtree, matching what the validation arm already traverses, and documented the parity openly. The parity was real. The choice was still a defect, and a cold review classified it a blocker.

The reasoning that produced it was: the two arms disagree on formal invalidity only, so giving them *different* descendant notions would be a second unmeasured claim. That inverts the cost. Refusing an unmeasured shape asserts nothing and stays correctable. Counting it asserts a value for a model plus cells that no observation covers, and does so through the very preflight that was supposed to be the explicit boundary. A negative existential is cheap to retract; a value is not.

The owning gap said so in words: *do not widen the compute-side presence account past a fixed nonrepeatable operand whose descendants are direct fields without measuring the wider shape*, and it listed nested descendants among the shapes the observation does not cover. Three documents agreed. None was consulted at the moment the descendant filter was written.

What generalizes:

1. **When an owning gap forbids widening without measurement, consistency with a sibling mechanism is not an exemption.** The sibling's scope was measured *for the sibling*. Inheriting its shape inherits its scope claim too, and nobody measured that transfer.
2. **Prefer the boundary that says nothing.** Between two accounts where one refuses and one answers, and no observation separates them, the refusal is not the conservative-but-lesser option — it is the only one that is not a claim.
3. **A docstring that states a widening does not license it.** Naming the parity and adding "the retained observation covers direct fields only" read as disclosure, but disclosure of an undischarged claim is still the claim. Compare `LF84`: writing the guard is not consulting it.

The through-line with `LF75`, `LF84`, and `LF85` holds again, one step further out. Those were failures to consult a guard about a claim's *discharge*. This is a failure to consult a guard about a claim's *scope*, made while documenting the exact sentence that should have triggered it.

## LF87 — reusing a family's membership predicate adopts the scope claim of every arm, and the arm that names a container is where an unmeasured expansion enters

The entry audit for the scalar `numeric` leaf's reference projection had two named preconditions: whether the fragment can carry a starred operand, and whether its `referencesField` is exhaustive. Both answers were the expected ones and both were cheap to establish at the type level — `ResolvedNumericAtom` has twelve constructors, none carrying a star path, and every predicate in the chain covers every constructor with no catch-all. On that basis the leaf was implemented as a one-line reuse of the existing sieve, which classifies a family by filtering the model's declarations through the family's own predicate.

That implementation was wrong, and the closure assessment caught it, not the audit. Sieving reuses a predicate **whole**, so it adopts the meaning of every arm at once. Ten arms name resolved fields or fixed field lists, where adopting the meaning is adopting field identity. The eleventh is a fixed group count, whose arm reports *every field in the counted subtree by path prefix* — and that meaning was established for the static error-field reference gate. `spec/01` specifies recursive descendant expansion for a **starred** group only, and the owning gap already refuses the group-list leaf's unstarred group operand because the fixed case is unmeasured. The sieve answered that open question positively, in passing, with no case naming it.

The same arm also falsifies a second inference that felt safe: *this is the scalar fragment, every field atom must be nonrepeatable under `sameGroup` admission, therefore every reference it projects is coordinate-free.* `fixedWellFormedBool` admits a `RuleGroup`-origin count operand whose path is the rule's own group even when that group is repeatable, so the leaf can reference fields inside a repeatable scope — and a deeper repeatable descendant in the same subtree is not bindable by the firing environment at all. Both wrong inferences ran through the one container-valued constructor.

Three things generalize:

1. **A sieve is not a neutral reuse of a predicate; it is an adoption of every arm's semantics.** Gate it with an exhaustive per-constructor sievability test, so that a container-valued arm and any constructor added later must choose explicitly rather than default into the answer. A `Bool` classifier written as a total match is enough, and it is the difference between refusing and asserting.
2. **Audit the closure of a membership predicate, not the admission of its constructors.** The operand gates bound what an operand *is*; they say nothing about what its predicate *reports*, and the gap between the two is exactly one constructor wide — the one naming a container instead of a leaf. Every family with a group-, list-, or subtree-valued operand has this gap.
3. **A fragment's scalar-ness is a claim about its reads, not about its addresses.** "Scalar" was established for evaluation, where it is true and load-bearing. Carrying it into the reference channel imported a coordinate claim evaluation had never made.

The mechanism is `LF86` one layer down and worth naming as such: there the widening was written deliberately and disclosed; here it arrived through a reuse whose whole appeal was that it added no new account. That is the more dangerous form, because there is no moment at which the claim is authored. The tell was available and cheap — the shared iteration model could not author the count shape at all, so the fixture had to be built specially, and "why does this need a new model?" is exactly the question whose answer was "because an arm you have not thought about is reachable".

**Addendum, same day.** The specific question was then measured: an unstarred group operand *does* expand recursively, with concrete coordinates, identically in all three operand positions, so the count's arm was right and the gate is deleted. That does not retire the lesson, and the sequence is the point. Refusing cost one guarded case and a request; it produced a measurement that now covers three carriers at once. Had the sieve simply answered, the same three carriers would have shipped on an undischarged claim that read as settled, and nothing would have prompted anyone to ask. The method survives its own vindicated arm: gate the sieve, file the question, delete the gate when the answer arrives.

## LF88 — a refusal written into a plan is an instruction to write a refusal into the code, so a planning document needs the discharge table too

`SG5` recorded, as one of four design constraints settled before any code, that `FieldValuesNotUnique` "would silently inherit an operand shape no measurement admits for it, so it must refuse a group operand at its own whole-list gate and project `none`", and [`PLAN.md`](PLAN.md) item 6 then scoped "`FieldValuesNotUnique`'s refusal of the shape" into the selected next unit. One upstream measurement refuted both halves: the kernel admits the shape, on a non-repeatable group and on a starred repeatable one, with the equivalent explicit lists passing as controls in the same run. Had the capsule started before the handback arrived, it would have shipped an over-rejection that makes a kernel-legal model unrepresentable and, on an inbound conversion path, unreadable.

Every guard needed to stop it was already written, and none of them looks here. The discharge table in [`CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md) classifies the claim exactly: reachability, discharged by **measurement only**, where reading never discharges it; and it separately forbids the second half in its own sentence, never let reachability gate a semantic clause. `check-spec-sync.sh` reads `spec/`. [`LF84`](#lf84--a-handbacks-claims-enter-spec-through-an-unguarded-door-and-the-discharge-table-was-written-only-for-claims-going-out) built a door for claims arriving from the peer. All three are oriented at normative text or at the repository boundary, and this claim crossed neither: it sat in a gap ledger and a plan, one fresh context away from being implemented verbatim.

Two things generalize:

1. **A refusal in a planning document is not a description, it is an instruction.** A `spec/` clause is read by someone who can disagree with it; a scope item gets implemented. So "it must refuse X" carries a strictly higher obligation in `SEMANTICS-GAPS.md` or `PLAN.md` than the same sentence carries in a clause, and the phrasing rule already written for `spec/` applies unchanged: write "no witness known as of `<rev>`", never "no measurement admits it". The first invites the measurement that settles it; the second reads as though it already happened.
2. **The tell is a negative existential doing work.** This claim's only support was the absence of a row, and its only consequence was a refusal. Where those two meet, the weakest evidence class is justifying the most irreversible representation choice, and the error is invisible by construction: an unmodeled legal input produces no failing case, because refusing to represent it looks exactly like representing it correctly until someone measures.

The through-line with [`LF84`](#lf84--a-handbacks-claims-enter-spec-through-an-unguarded-door-and-the-discharge-table-was-written-only-for-claims-going-out), [`LF85`](#lf85--a-claim-already-sitting-in-this-projects-own-documents-is-not-thereby-discharged-and-restating-it-is-a-fresh-claim), and [`LF86`](#lf86--refusing-claims-nothing-extending-by-parity-claims-something-and-an-owning-gaps-do-not-widen-is-not-waived-by-a-sibling-arm) is theirs, moved one document to the left: the guards this project writes keep being pointed at where a claim is *published* rather than where it is *decided*.

## LF89 — finding a confound in an inbound claim does not leave the local clause standing, and the first thing to check is whether both trace to one source

An inbound handback stated that a bare unstarred repeatable group operand's admission depends on where the error field sits. Its fixture declared `indexFieldName: "Sku"` and its inside-the-group case used that very index field as the error field, so three candidate conditions co-varied and the eight cells could not pick locus out of them. Catching that was right, and filing it with the separating witness named was right. **The next inference was wrong.** `spec/07` held a clause on the same shape stating that index-keying is what admits the bare form, and the reply sent upstream said the inbound rows were "that clause's case, not a counterexample to it", treating the local clause as an independent account that survived the confound.

It was not independent. The peer measured the witness within one model differing in a single builder call, and **index-keying is not a gate at all**: the two matrices are identical with and without it. The local clause was the wrong one. Its provenance turned out to be the peer's own `KERNEL-FINDINGS`, which had carried the same un-separated conjunct, *"only when the group is INDEX-keyed ... and the error field sits INSIDE it"*, and handed it over years-of-reading ago. One fixture, one conjunct nobody separated, propagated into three documents across two projects.

Two things generalize:

1. **A confound means nobody knows, including us.** The natural reading of "your rows cannot separate these three" is that the inbound claim is unsupported, which invites treating the incumbent local clause as the surviving account. But a confound removes support symmetrically: it says the discriminator was never run, so every clause resting on that fixture is equally unsupported, whichever direction it points. The correct posture on catching one is to suspend both accounts, which is what the filed gap did and what the reply then undercut.
2. **Check provenance before treating a local clause as corroboration or as a defence.** The reply called the local clause "a different route". One `git grep` of [`SOURCES.md`](SOURCES.md) for where that sentence entered would have shown it arrived from the same peer, and a claim cannot corroborate itself by having been copied. This is the [`LF85`](#lf85--a-claim-already-sitting-in-this-projects-own-documents-is-not-thereby-discharged-and-restating-it-is-a-fresh-claim) rule read in the other direction: `LF85` says restating a local claim is a fresh claim, and this says citing one against an inbound claim is not a second witness when it *is* the inbound claim, older.

The episode's own outcome is the argument for the method rather than against it, and worth keeping attached: declining the confounded section is what caused the witness to be run, and the correction that came back was to this project's clause. Refusing to absorb a claim you cannot separate is not conservatism that protects your existing text; it is the move that finds out which text was wrong.

**Addendum, next round, and it is the same lesson caught one step earlier.** The very batch that corrected the clause above carried a second confounded row: an inbound `FirstFilledValue` result reported `DATE` beside `DATE_FRAGMENT` as refused, against a control of two same-kind operands, and it was absorbed here as a genuine per-carrier disagreement with `FieldValuesNotUnique`'s format-keyed gate. `spec/07` briefly gained a marker saying the two carriers disagree. They do not: the refused row varied **kind and declared format together**, and separated, both carriers key on the format.

**The defeater was again local, and this time it was in the sentence being written.** The reply that recorded the disagreement cited our own format-keyed clause as the disagreeing party. That clause names declared format as the variable this operator's gate turns on, which is exactly the variable the inbound row failed to hold constant. So the tell generalizes: **when an inbound row disagrees with a local clause, the disagreement is itself the hypothesis to test, because the local clause names the variable the inbound row may not have controlled.** A disagreement between a measurement and a clause is not two accounts to record side by side; it is a prompt to check whether the two are even varying the same thing. Recording it as "both measured, neither generalizes" reads as careful and was, here, the way the confound got written into the spec.

Two absorbed confounds in two rounds, each arriving with a control attached, each control holding constant precisely the variable that mattered. A control is not evidence that a row is separated; it is evidence about the one axis it varies, and the question to ask of every inbound table is which axes moved together in it.

**Second addendum: the confound has two siblings, and the discharge table already owns the class it was missing.** The peer generalized this round's lesson further, and the generalization holds. A **confound** is a fixture that cannot separate two variables. A **sample-based absence** is a search that cannot establish a negative: its next draft concluded that two temporal kinds "each admit exactly one format" from probing six and five candidates and seeing one acceptance each, and the true set was already enumerated in its own findings. A **sweep resting on an unmeasured cardinality** is a completeness argument that cannot support its own scope: a sibling sweep of that kind had declared a defect class closed while excluding an entire kind, invisibly, for three months, because a sweep reads as diligence.

All three look like evidence, travel with evidence's authority, and are missing exactly the part that would let them carry the weight placed on them. The useful part for this project is that [`CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md)'s discharge table already classifies the second and third correctly, as reachability and scope claims discharged by **measurement only** where reading never discharges them. What it does not do is name the two forms they actually arrive in, which are never phrased as "no legal model can express this": they arrive as *"I probed the candidates and only one worked"* and as *"the sweep covered the class"*. Those are the sentences to treat as the forbidden phrasing, because they are what the forbidden phrasing looks like in a working note.

## LF90 — a routing answer is a provenance claim, and the document that *names* a route is not the document that *owns* it

> Date: 2026-08-15. Sections: cross-cutting. Basis: a wrong reply to the user about where a `dmtool` instrument defect is recorded, corrected by the user in the same session.

Asked where a `dmtool` instrument defect goes, this session answered "a dated feedback note in the spec-sync ledger". The first half was right and the second was invented. [`TESTING.md`](TESTING.md#structured-dmtool-probes-and-feedback) owns the contract: one new dated Markdown note per working session in the user's `~/Downloads` exchange directory — the only permitted file-exchange location — updated after each probe capsule with new findings or an explicit no-new-defect result, immutable once handed off, sequenced `-02` for a later same-day finding, and its location reported to the user.

**The mechanism is a provenance claim stated without discharge, and the defeater was in the sentence being read.** The paragraph in front of the session was the ledger's own [kinds of entry](archived/A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER-THROUGH-2026-08-28.md#kinds-of-entry), which says the defect "goes to a dated feedback note **per `TESTING.md`**". That pointer is the disclaimer that the ledger is not the owner. The phrase was taken from it and the pointer was not followed, so the note was placed in the document where its *name* appeared rather than the one that defines it. [`CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md)'s discharge table already classifies this exactly — provenance, discharged by confirming the cited source states *this* fact and not an adjacent one — but the table was being applied only to semantic claims, when it says plainly that a **reply to the user** is a claim like any other.

Two rules follow, both cheap:

1. **A routing or procedure answer gets the discharge table.** "X is recorded as Y" is a provenance claim about this repository's own rules, and one `grep` of the owning section discharges it. Answering it from working memory is the same act as extending a short revision into a plausible full SHA: fluent, adjacent, and wrong in the one detail that decides what gets written where.
2. **When document A cites document B for a fact, B is the owner and A is not evidence.** A citation is a hand-off, and reading the citing paragraph is reading the hand-off rather than the thing handed off. The tell is literally the word "per".

The aggravating condition is worth naming because it will recur: the question arrived mid-turn, during a proof, and was answered at conversational speed. Nothing was gained by the speed — the user was not blocked — and the cost was a correction round plus a session in which a stated rule was wrong. A procedural question asked mid-task is not a reason to answer from memory; it is a reason to say so and pay the lookup. The through-line with [`LF85`](#lf85--a-claim-already-sitting-in-this-projects-own-documents-is-not-thereby-discharged-and-restating-it-is-a-fresh-claim) and [`LF88`](#lf88--a-refusal-written-into-a-plan-is-an-instruction-to-write-a-refusal-into-the-code-so-a-planning-document-needs-the-discharge-table-too) is theirs again, one surface further out: the guards keep being pointed at where a claim is published, and this one was decided in a chat reply that no gate reads.

**Addendum, same session, and it is the half the first version missed.** The account above is agent-side only — "follow the pointer" — and treated as sufficient. It is not, because it does not explain why *this* route was the one reached by carelessness. The routing sentence in [`CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md)'s upstream rule dispatches **four** channels, and three carried their destination: `EXP-` and `SPEC-` name the ledger and link to it, and the proposal route resolves to "a temporary proposal document under this repository's `docs/`" one hop on. The `dmtool` feedback note was the only one whose destination was recoverable from neither the routing sentence nor its own link, and both addressless statements sat beside a link to the ledger. A `grep` for the artifact's name hit the two addressless statements before the owner. **A routing entry that names a channel without its address, next to a link to a different destination, is a defect in the routing table and not only in its reader** — and the discriminator that proves it is the three sibling routes that were never mis-taken.

Repaired at both addressless loci by carrying the destination and the explicit negative into them: the routing sentence and the ledger's own [kinds of entry](archived/A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER-THROUGH-2026-08-28.md#kinds-of-entry) now say `~/Downloads` and "never this ledger", while [`TESTING.md`](TESTING.md#structured-dmtool-probes-and-feedback) keeps the full contract. The negative belongs specifically in the ledger, because that is the document an agent is already reading when it makes the mistake. The general form is worth keeping: **when a rule names an artifact, check that the same sentence or its link resolves to where the artifact goes; if a sibling route in the same rule carries its address and this one does not, the asymmetry is the bug.**

## LF91 — an operand-level accessor is a hidden claim that one operand has one declaration, and widening an operand to a *scope* turns every such accessor into a soundness question

> Date: 2026-08-15. Sections: §9 repetition and iteration; §5 numeric authoring. Basis: retaining a group-scope slot on the shared checked Number entity operand; `spec/07` group-operand clauses; the per-cell directional-missingness rule in `Semantics/NumericAggregate.lean`.

Until this capsule every checked entity-list slot denoted exactly one declaration, so accessors of the shape `operand → Bool` and `operand → MessagePointer` were correct by construction and read as neutral plumbing. A group slot denotes a **scope**, and each of those accessors turned out to encode the old assumption as a claim.

Two came due at once and they failed differently, which is the useful part. `referencePointer` returned **one** pointer per slot; a group contributes its whole expansion, so the type itself was wrong and the compiler said so. `declarationSigned` returned **one** Bool per slot and kept compiling — but the semantics reads directional missingness from each *empty cell's own* declaration, while the aggregate scan applies the operand-level Bool to every cell of that operand's side. A group spanning declarations that disagree therefore had no correct answer, and the wrong one would have been silent: a signed field's missing cell rendered with an unsigned neighbour's direction, in a fold nothing type-checks against.

**The tell is that the accessor's result domain does not scale with the operand's extent.** A `List MessagePointer` scales; a single `MessagePointer` does not. A per-cell fact scales; a per-operand `Bool` standing in for it does not. Where the domain does not scale, the accessor was a *specialisation* of a per-element fact that happened to be valid while extents were singletons, and widening the operand is exactly when that stops being true.

Lean treatment: the retained slot carries a `uniformSigned` obligation, so the operand-level accessor is sound for every representable group and the shape it cannot answer is refused rather than guessed. That refusal is explicitly a **representation** limit and not a Kernel gate — the Kernel very likely admits the mixed shape — and it is recorded as such in [`SG5`](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md#sg5--numeric-authoring-and-target-completion) with its widening trigger, which is the runtime capsule that makes signedness per-cell. Refusing an unmeasured shape while a wrong answer would have been invisible is the right trade here, and it is the same posture [`LF88`](#lf88--a-refusal-written-into-a-plan-is-an-instruction-to-write-a-refusal-into-the-code-so-a-planning-document-needs-the-discharge-table-too) warns about only when the refusal is dressed up as a Kernel class; unprojected, it claims nothing.

The general rule for the remaining carriers, which will each repeat this: **before widening an operand's extent, list every accessor on it and ask whether the result domain is per-operand or per-element.** The ones the compiler catches are free; the ones it does not are the reason to make the list.

**Addendum, same session, third instance, and this one was in code rather than prose.** Having added a `.group` refusal arm to nine aggregate resolvers, this session wrote into [`IMPLEMENTATION-MAP.md`](IMPLEMENTATION-MAP.md), [`SEMANTICS-GAPS.md`](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md), and [`PLAN.md`](PLAN.md) that "the value aggregates still refuse a group operand", and committed it. They did not. The checked-document aggregate entry point does not match on the operand at all — it delegates to the resolver that had just been opened — so `Sum` over a group had been evaluating correctly, and unguarded, since the previous commit. One `#eval` returned `12` and refuted three documents at once.

**A negative behavioral claim is a value claim and needs the same executable case a positive one does.** [`CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md)'s discharge table already says so; what hid it is that "X refuses" feels like the absence of behavior rather than behavior, so it gets written as prose in a status document while every positive claim in the same capsule gets an `example`. The capsule had cases for what the group operand *does* and a sentence for what it *does not*, and only the sentence was wrong.

The specific trap is worth naming because it will recur in any capsule that opens a shape across a family: **adding refusal arms to N functions does not establish that the feature refuses, only that those N arms do.** The question the arms cannot answer is which of them the entry points actually reach, and it is answered by running the entry point, not by counting the arms. The tell was available and free — the refusal was never given a case, in a session that wrote cases for everything else.

**Addendum, the token carrier, and the audit paid for itself.** The same accessor list run on `CheckedTokenEntityOperand` found `tokenOperand : FlatTextFieldOperand` — a per-*element* fact standing in for a whole slot, and one the compiler would not have caught, because a group's cells type-check against any operand.
