It is not a latent hazard either: `FlatTextFieldOperand.checkedValueListCellAt` dispatches on String versus Enumeration and, for Enumeration, through *that declaration's* resolved projection. A group expansion containing an Enumeration and a String — legal, and admitted by `NumberOfDifferentValues`, which welcomes that mix — would have read one of the two through the other's classifier. The conformance module projects the same resolved slot both ways: per declaration it is `[present "A", present "A"]`, through the first slot's operand it is `[present "A", unknown]`.

The fix generalises the shape of the earlier one. `referencePointer` became `referencePointers` because a slot contributes many pointers; here `tokenOperand` became `tokenOperands` **and** the resolved operand stopped carrying a flat cell list at all, because pairing recovered afterwards by field-identifier lookup would need a total fallback for a cell that cannot occur. The walk that produces the extent hands each cell back beside its declaration's payload, so the association is structural. That is the durable move: **when the per-element fact cannot be recovered from the widened result, widen the producer rather than the accessor.**

One accessor came out sound for a reason worth recording, because it looks like the same hazard and is not. A group slot carries no `FieldEntityReadForm` — `SurfaceFieldEntityOperand.group` simply has no such field where `.field` does — so every member is read stored. That is a consequence of the authored *surface*, not an unmeasured guess about the Kernel, and it is proved rather than asserted (`checkedTokenEntityGroup_projections_stored`). Distinguishing "the surface cannot express it" from "no measurement covers it" is what keeps the first from being filed as a gap and the second from being filed as a fact.

Retained cases now lock all four aggregate operators over the extent, and the boundary that genuinely does refuse — computation — has a case of its own rather than a sentence.

## LF92 — a signature adopted as a structural guard encodes the *opposite* error just as silently, and two channels of one operand are the cheapest test that either is wrong

> Date: 2026-08-15. Sections: §9 repetition and iteration; §3 data model. Basis: the group-operand extent resolver and the `referenced` projection; a12-dmkits `bffe9cca` and `c1eb1614`; `spec/07`'s scope trap marker.

`spec/07` marks the group-operand scope rule as an observed contract and warns that reading the compared extent off the rule's own iterating group or binding depth gets it wrong. This project took that seriously in the strongest available way: `resolveCheckedGroupEntityOperandCore` was given **no environment argument at all**, and three documents recorded the absent parameter as the structural guard — a rule you cannot break because the input is not there.

It was half a rule. The correct depth is the **operand's** own, which is neither the rule's nor nothing: levels above the operand stay bound, levels at and below are free. Reading the rule's depth pins an operand that names an ancestor; reading no depth widens a star on an inner level under a *bound* outer one to rows the star never reopened. A fixture proves it — `FieldValuesNotUnique(/Form/Outer/Inner*)` evaluated from `Outer[1]` fired on a duplicate lying only across `Outer` rows.

**Making a parameter unavailable prevents exactly one derivation and endorses another.** The guard is only as good as the claim that the excluded input is the *only* wrong one, and nothing in the design records that claim as a claim. A totality obligation or a named depth would have carried it; an absent argument carried the reasoning in prose and lost it.

The second half is a defect *detector* worth generalizing. A group operand has two channels — the `(row × field)` set it compares and the `referenced` set it publishes — and both answer "how far does this operand reach". The peer fixed its reference channel, then found the compared set disagreed with it, and its own note says the disagreement was the cheapest available signal. Both now read one `CheckedEntityGroupSource.boundLevelCount`, so they cannot diverge. **Wherever one construct answers the same structural question through two channels, agreement between them is a free oracle, and shared derivation is how you keep it.** This is the same shape as the reference channel's own case: neither channel was checked against a kernel row on this axis, yet the pair was still decisive about one of them being wrong.

The third half is about the separating matrix, and it is the uncomfortable one. Every fixture in this family — the Number extent rows, the token rows, the peer's own twenty-eight — has **no repeatable level above the operand**. The whole matrix was therefore silent on the distinction, and each new case added confidence without adding coverage. The tell was available for free: the depth rule names three positions (above, at, below) and the fixtures only ever populated two. **When a rule is stated in terms of a boundary, check that some fixture sits on each side of it**; a matrix that is exhaustive over the cases anyone thought to write is not exhaustive over the cases the rule distinguishes.

## LF93 — a fixture that encodes "this project does not support it" as "the system does not allow it" moves when legality tightens, and the same conflation hides inside a validity law

> Date: 2026-08-22. Sections: §5 dates and time; §9 computations. Basis: the DateRange declaration allowlist in `spec/05` §8; the reviewed a12-dmkits checkpoint retaining the complete 8x4 grid; `DateRangeDeclarationPolicy.error?` and four conformance modules.

Replacing a source-presence check with the Kernel-measured eight-pair allowlist was a three-line semantic change that broke four conformance modules, and not one of them was testing declaration legality. Each had needed a DateRange policy that this project cannot parse, and each had reached for a pair the Kernel does not admit either: ISO format with a dash, dotted format with a slash. Under a presence-only gate those pairs were indistinguishable from legal ones, so they served as "unsupported" stand-ins for as long as the gate stayed weak.

**The failure mode is not a wrong expectation but a relocated one.** The affected examples did not report a changed value; the model stopped preparing, so every case in the file failed at once and the actual assertions were never reached. A fixture built on an illegal configuration tests whichever gate happens to reject it first, and that is not a stable property of the fixture.

The fix is available in the same measurement that broke it. The allowlist admits two pairs this project has no parser for, so "legal, and unsupported here" is now expressible directly: `MM` with the empty separator and `dd.MM` with dash. Every relocated fixture moved onto one of them and became sharper than before, because the separator-only mismatch case is now authorable within the legal set — month-only is the sole format admitting two separators, which no full-Date pair could have shown.

The same conflation had reached a proved law. `dateRangeDeclarationPolicy_valid_sources_nonempty`, since removed with the account it encoded, stated that an admitted declaration retains a nonempty format **and** a nonempty separator, which reads as structural and is false: the Kernel admits exactly one empty separator. The presence check and the theorem were the same claim written twice, so the measurement refuted both together. **A validity law whose conjuncts are read off the current gate inherits every scope assumption of that gate**, and the tell is that the law's proof cites nothing but the gate's own definition.

Two cheap habits follow. When a fixture needs "unsupported", name which layer refuses it and prefer a configuration the modeled system accepts; if none exists yet, say so in the fixture comment rather than borrowing an illegal one. And when a static gate is widened or narrowed, grep the laws proved from it before the fixtures: a relocated fixture fails loudly, while a law that quietly keeps proving a weaker fact does not.

## LF94 — an enum that names a component shape becomes the key a renderer trusts, so adding a lexically distinct member to it emits known-wrong text rather than no text

> Date: 2026-08-22. Sections: §5 dates and time; §9 computations. Basis: `DateRangeInputFormat`, its target renderer, and the two declaration pairs added by the Kernel-measured allowlist.

Two of the eight admitted DateRange declarations, `MM` with an empty separator and `dd.MM` with dash, retain exactly the components their slash-separated siblings retain. Reading only the parser, the obvious move is to map them onto the existing `yearlessMonth` and `yearlessMonthDay` constructors and thread the declared separator through the parse, which is what the existing fragment parsers already do. Every stored-input case would have passed.

**The renderer is what makes that wrong, and it is three modules away from the parser.** `renderYearlessMonth` and `renderYearlessMonthDay` take the shape constructor and hardcode `"/"` and `"-"` inside the rendered text; they never see the policy. So a shared constructor would have rendered `06/09` where the Kernel stores `0609`, and `06-01/09-30` where it stores `01.06-30.09` — both of them values this project has retained from both Kernel strategies. The failure would not have been an unmodeled gap but a confidently wrong output on a measured row.

The general shape: **a classifier written for one consumer becomes the key every later consumer trusts, and a formatter is the consumer that turns an under-specified key into a wrong answer instead of a missing one.** A parser can discard information harmlessly, because what it discards is already in the input; a renderer must reconstruct it, so any distinction the key drops gets invented. Whenever a new member is added to a classification, check the *widest* consumer of that classification before the nearest one, and ask what each consumer must reconstruct from the key alone.

Two smaller notes worth keeping. The `nonempty` field of every other rendered range proves itself by `simp`, because a literal separator sits in the middle of the concatenation; the one presentation with no separator has to reach for the component renderer's own nonemptiness, which is the cheapest possible signal that this arm is lexically unlike its siblings. And a totality obligation did the real work here: `renderResolved` returns a `StoredDateRange` rather than an `Option`, so a new constructor could not be left unhandled, and the choice between rendering it correctly and changing the signature had to be made deliberately. **A total function over an extended enum forces the question that an optional one would have let a `none` swallow.**

## LF95 — an admission question is answerable by the runtime probe, because its refusal path *is* the consistency oracle; routing by how a question is phrased filed an experiment request for something measurable in twenty minutes

> Date: 2026-08-22. Sections: §5 dates and time; §9 computations. Basis: `EXP-2026-08-22-01`, filed and resolved the same day; `KERNEL-PROBE-SPEC` §5; `TESTING.md`'s probe-route rule.

The question was whether the kernel admits a DateRange computation whose source and target declare the same date components under different spellings. It arrived looking like a *mechanism* question — a peer docstring citing `hasSameComponents`, one measured crossing consistent with two rival readings — and mechanism questions in this project usually mean an upstream request. So it became an `EXP-` entry. It should not have: **`computation add --dry-run` is kernel-gated, and `:adapter:kernelProbe` refuses to write when the consistency oracle reports an `ERROR`**, which makes both instruments admission oracles. Six crossings, two negative controls, and four runtime values took about twenty minutes.

**The routing rule this project already had is "check the maintained route first", and what defeated it was the question's phrasing rather than its content.** A static-legality question sounds like something you settle by reading source, and the probe advertises itself as a *runtime* instrument, so the two never met. The fix is to route by what the instruments can observe: `KERNEL-PROBE-SPEC` §5 spells out that an inadmissible model fails the run with its diagnostics, which is exactly an admission verdict, and the same is true of every kernel-gated dry-run. Ask "can any instrument distinguish these accounts", never "is this a runtime or a source question".

Two details worth carrying forward. **A negative control is what turns an accepted dry-run into evidence.** An accepted `computation add --dry-run` reports `ok:true` with `verification:KERNEL_CONFIRMED` and *no* `valid` key at all, and `TESTING.md` warns in the same breath that a missing `valid` is the signature of a child that established nothing — so acceptance alone is ambiguous between "the gate said yes" and "no gate ran". Running a known-bad crossing through the identical command shape resolved it: it returned `ok:false, valid:false` with `MVK_INVALID_COMPARE_TO_DATE_RANGE`, proving the gate was live and discriminating on that exact axis.

And **the wrong account had already been locked as four executable cases**, one commit earlier, on the grounds that carrier equality was the conservative choice. It was not conservative; it refused four legal models. The tell was available before the measurement: the refusals traced to an *implementation artifact* — a shared `carrier` index in the checked shape — rather than to any source-grounded rule, and nothing in the account cited an authority. **A refusal inherited from a type's shape deserves the same discharge as a claim, because that is what it is.**

## LF96 — a case that pins a refusal cause pins a path through ordered gates, so inserting an earlier gate silently retargets it, and can make a downstream cause unreachable without anyone deciding that

> Date: 2026-08-22. Sections: §5 dates and time. Basis: `mixesDateRangeYearInclusion` replacing `hasMeasuredYearlessDateRangeMix`; the mixed-year checkpoint; `Conformance.DateRangeOverlapOperators`' unsupported-policy case.

Generalizing the DateRange overlap year gate from an enumerated two-operand shape to a property of the whole operand list was the intended change, and it is a fourth instance of [LF95](#lf95--an-admission-question-is-answerable-by-the-runtime-probe-because-its-refusal-path-is-the-consistency-oracle-routing-by-how-a-question-is-phrased-filed-an-experiment-request-for-something-measurable-in-twenty-minutes)'s class: the old predicate matched exactly two operands and two of the four yearless spellings, and every one of those bounds was an implementation artifact rather than a measured rule. What was not anticipated is what the wider gate did to a case three files away that had nothing to do with year classes.

**That case paired an exact operand with a yearless one to check that an unsupported declaration is refused explicitly rather than becoming an empty operand.** It had picked its yearless stand-in for the reason [LF93](#lf93--a-fixture-that-encodes-this-project-does-not-support-it-as-the-system-does-not-allow-it-moves-when-legality-tightens-and-the-same-conflation-hides-inside-a-validity-law) prescribes — legal to declare, unsupported on this route — so the fixture was correct. The new gate sits ahead of operand certification and rejects that same list first, so the case now observed the year diagnostic instead. The assertion was still about a real behaviour; it was just no longer about the behaviour it was written for.

The consequence is worth stating separately from the fix, because it is the part that does not announce itself: **after the gate, `unsupportedPolicy` on that route is reachable only from a uniformly yearless list.** Any mix trips the earlier gate, and a configured model admits everything, so an entire refusal path narrowed as a side effect of widening a different one. Nothing in the change requested that, and no gate would have failed if the case had happened to use a shape the new gate ignores.

Two habits follow. When inserting a gate ahead of existing ones, **grep the cases that assert the downstream causes, not only the ones that assert the new cause** — a case pinning `X` says "this input reaches X", which is a claim about the whole ordered chain. And when the reachable set of a cause narrows, record it where the route's boundary is described rather than leaving it implicit in the case that survived; a cause reachable from one shape only is a fact a consumer needs, and it is invisible from the error type.

A second note, about the representation rather than the cases. The mixed pair was expected to force a bridge between the two interval domains this family keeps — resolved instants and yearless month/day labels — and the plan was to design that join. The measurement dissolved the question instead: without a Base Year the Kernel refuses the mix statically, and with one it completes the yearless side into the resolved domain, so no legal input ever puts a value from each domain into the same comparison. **Before designing a join between two representation domains, check whether the authoring gate already keeps them apart** — a static refusal is a cheaper and more faithful answer than any bridge, and here it was also the measured one.

## LF97 — a narrowed view of an observation reads exactly like the complete observation, so the narrowing has to be ruled out rather than noticed

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: cross-cutting evidence method. Basis: two instances one day apart — the peer's diagnostic-multiplicity measurement at a12-dmkits `566edd6f1`, and a withdrawn `field read --text` defect report of this project's own; both recorded in the [instrument handback](SOURCES.md#src-dmtool-2026-08-22-instrument-handback).

Two measurement errors landed a day apart, and they are the same error with different authors.

**The tool narrowed, invisibly.** A static rule carrying two independent defects reports one diagnostic: the kernel suppresses the second. A 30-cell admission matrix run here used a non-operand error field for every row, so eight rows carried a live second objection that never appeared. The retained rows looked complete and were internally consistent, and the only reason the recorded claims survived is that they were re-run with the error field referenced before being written down — a habit, not a deduction. The peer's other row is what makes the class two-sided: one defect can also yield *two* codes. So a diagnostic count is not a defect count in either direction, and a single code never establishes that only one gate objected.

**Then this project narrowed, invisibly, and reported the artifact as a peer defect.** `field read --text` on a DATE_RANGE emits eleven lines, the eleventh being `interpretationOfYear: FROM`. The probe piped it through `head -10`. The result was a well-formed, plausible, complete-looking config block with the key absent, beside a JSON read where the key was present — which reads exactly like a renderer that drops it. That became a filed defect report with a reproducer, and cost the peer a measurement round and a mutation-proven regression case for a renderer that was never broken.

**The reason this class is dangerous is that both narrowings produce a *coherent* artifact.** Neither an error nor a gap: a smaller true thing, self-consistent, indistinguishable from the whole. Ordinary care does not catch it, and it survived a probe pass that did check `--version`, did cross-check the JSON channel, and did re-run rows to remove a confound — because none of those questions was "did I see all of the output".

Three guards, in increasing generality.

- **Never let a viewport command touch bytes that are the observation.** No `head`, `tail`, `cut`, or `| head -n` on a probe result. Read the whole envelope, or project it through a structural reader that fails loudly on a missing key. The tell is deliberately weak: a `head -N` returning exactly N lines is not evidence that N was the end, which is why it must be a rule rather than a thing to watch for.
- **When the instrument may narrow, vary one input at a time.** Attribute a diagnostic only from a row where nothing else was also wrong, and re-run any row whose error field or operand was not itself the subject.
- **Before reporting an external defect, re-run the exact command with every local transform removed.** A defect report is a claim leaving this repository, so it takes the discharge its class requires — and for a *value* claim about an observed output, the pipeline that produced the output is part of the input. That check costs one command and is the only step that would have caught this.

## LF98 — a law saying a declaration field is inert will not state cleanly against a do-notation entry point, and the proof that refuses to go through is naming a decomposition that was already due

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: §5 dates and time. Basis: `interpretationOfYear` in [`DateRangeInput.lean`](../A12Kernel/Elaboration/DateRangeInput.lean); the two extracted owners `completionYears` and `resolveYearlessForModel`; the [interpretation checkpoint](SOURCES.md#src-date-range-year-interpretation).

The measured claim was a widening: an optional declaration key makes a wrapping yearless range formally valid, and leaves every ordered range exactly as it was. The second half is the one a consumer needs, because it is what says that adding the key to a model cannot silently re-place an existing valid value. So the law to prove was inertness: two policies differing only in that field classify the same text identically.

**Stating it against the classifier that reads the field did not work, and the reason is structural rather than tactical.** `classifyStoredDateRangeForModel` is a `do` block that dispatches on seven input profiles. After `unfold`, both sides of the equation carry the whole elaborated continuation — `__do_jp` applications, a `pure`-bind that blocks reduction, and one copy of every sibling branch — while the actual difference sits four matches deep in two of the seven arms. Closing it needed a congruence lemma for each sibling the field does not reach: the exact route, the two year-bearing fragment routes, the format lookup, and the error payload that mentions the record twice. Every one of those lemmas would have been true and none of them would have been about the interpretation.

**Extracting the step that consumes the field turned the same law into three lines of `simp`.** `completionYears` owns the placement rule and `resolveYearlessForModel` owns the completion; inertness is then `simp` on one hypothesis, and the companion law — that the standard reading refuses every wrapping value under either Base Year state — falls out of the same two definitions. The generalizable form: **prove a field-inertness or field-irrelevance law at the smallest definition that reads the field, never at the entry point that dispatches to it.** A congruence proof across an elaborated `do` block pays for every branch the field never touches.

The part worth keeping is what the extraction did to the code, independently of the proof. The classification arm had grown to a five-deep nest of `match`, `if`, and monadic binds holding four separate decisions: decode, wrap detection, year placement, and zone resolution. It built cleanly and no gate objected. The proof was the first thing that objected, and it objected for the same reason a reader would. **When a law about a newly added field will not state cleanly, read it as a decomposition signal before reaching for tactics** — here the resistance and the code smell were one fact, and treating the tactic problem as the whole problem would have bought the same theorem plus four lemmas and left the nest in place.

Limit: this says nothing about proving laws that genuinely span a dispatch, where the entry point's own totality or ordering is the claim. Those belong at the entry point, and their cost is the price of the statement rather than a sign of misplacement.

## LF99 — a fallback that reports the first route's error swallows the second route's own refusal, so a gate added to both routes becomes invisible on exactly the paths that need the fallback

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: §5 dates and time. Basis: `certifyPluralScalar` and the plural list `.field` arm before and after the `interpretationOfYear` overlap gate; the [interpretation-overlap checkpoint](SOURCES.md#src-date-range-interpretation-overlap-refusal); `Conformance.DateRangeFragmentOverlap`'s plural refusal rows.

Three overlap certification dispatchers had the same shape: try the exact canonical policy, and if that fails try the direct fragment profile. Two of them ended `| .error _ => throw canonicalError` — the fragment route's own refusal was discarded and the canonical one reported in its place. That was defensible when it was written, because the only reason a legal DateRange declaration reached the fragment route was that its policy was not canonical, and the canonical `unsupportedPolicy` was the cause the operator's diagnostics already named.

**Adding a declaration-level gate to both routes broke it silently.** A measured Kernel refusal — an `interpretationOfYear`-bearing declaration is not a legal overlap operand — belongs in both routes, since either can be the one that certifies a given declaration. The singular dispatcher, which fell back only on `.unsupportedPolicy` and rethrew anything else, reported the new cause correctly. The two swallowing dispatchers reported `unsupportedPolicy` with the old format and separator: a true statement about the canonical attempt, and a completely misleading answer to why the operand was refused. Both cases were red for the right reason and would have been green under a laxer assertion, because the operand *was* refused — only the cause was wrong.

The generalizable rule is about what a fallback's condition means. **Key a fallback on the specific cause that means "this route does not apply", never on failure in general, and let every other cause propagate.** A catch-all fallback conflates "the first route was inapplicable" with "the second route refused on its own grounds", and the conflation is invisible until someone adds a reason of the second kind. Its cost is not a wrong verdict but a wrong explanation, which is worse in a project whose consumers read the cause to decide what to change in a model.

Two corollaries worth keeping.

- **When several dispatchers implement the same try-then-fallback, they must share the fallback condition, not merely the shape.** Here two of three swallowed and one did not, and the difference showed up as an inconsistent diagnostic across two operators that the Kernel treats identically. Unifying them was a smaller change than the gate itself.
- **A refusal case should assert the cause, not just the refusal.** `isNone = true` on the certification would have passed throughout and hidden the whole defect. The rows that caught it name the exact constructor and, separately, the Kernel diagnostic it maps to.

## LF100 — a refusal names an entity, not the gate's subject; only an admitted row that varies the entity says which one the gate reads

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: §13. Basis: the [semantic-index checkpoint](SOURCES.md#src-rule-message-semantic-index) at kernel `30.8.1`, and the gate it corrected in [`Elaboration/ValidationMessageAuthoring.lean`](../A12Kernel/Elaboration/ValidationMessageAuthoring.lean).

A rule message may key a parameter by a semantic index, and the Kernel's refusal reads: *a semantic index `Rows/Payload For "k1"` can be used in an error text only if it is used in the rule condition in the same way.* Three refusing rows agreed with it — a plain-read condition, a differently-keyed condition, and an unkeyed parameter against a keyed condition — and every one of them quoted a field together with a key. So the gate was implemented as a predicate over *(field, key)*: does the condition reference this field through this key.

That was wrong, and the row that says so is an **admission**: `$Rows/Key For "k1"$` is accepted against a condition that uses only `Rows/Payload For "k1"`. The keyed field is absent from the condition entirely. The gate's subject is the **index** — the group and the key — and the field's own membership is required only of an *unkeyed* parameter, which draws a different class (`INVALID_FIELD`) for its own separate reason.

**A refusal message quotes the whole offending expression, so every entity in it is a candidate subject and none is confirmed.** Refusals constrain the gate's *extension* jointly; they cannot factor it. Only varying one entity while holding the rest fixed, and getting an **admission**, isolates which entity the gate reads. This is [`LF80`](#lf80--a-separator-that-both-accounts-satisfy-is-not-a-separator-and-a-boolean-lock-hides-which-one-you-have)'s rule applied to a gate's subject rather than to a verdict: rows that all refuse are rows that all accounts predict.

Two consequences worth keeping.

- **A gate whose subject is wrong can still be unreachable-and-therefore-green.** The flat condition spine carries no keyed leaf, so the predicate was constant-false and every test passed under either account. What exposed it was measuring the admitted direction rather than re-reading the code; nothing internal could have. When a gate's own acceptance branch has no witness in the modeled fragment, its *subject* is exactly the part no local check can hold.
- **Prefer refusing at the fragment's own boundary over borrowing the Kernel's class.** The corrected form decodes a keyed parameter and refuses it as outside a nonrepeatable fragment, mapped to **no** Kernel diagnostic — because the Kernel's class answers a question this spine cannot pose. The first version mapped its refusal to `MVK_INDEX_FOR_ERROR_TEXT_INVALID` and so published a correspondence claim for inputs never measured. A fragment boundary is honest; a borrowed class is a claim.

## LF101 — a smart constructor that enforces a policy makes its own `none` ambiguous, so the caller cannot assign the right cause

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: §6. Basis: the [partial-cause checkpoint](SOURCES.md#src-partial-date-input-causes) at kernel `30.8.1`, and the defect it caught in [`PartialDateInput.lean`](../A12Kernel/Elaboration/PartialDateInput.lean).

`OmittedDayDate.ofYearMonth?` builds a partially known Date's two boundaries and returns `none` unless both completions are real **and** satisfy the universal Gregorian floor. Bundling the floor into the constructor is good hygiene: no caller can hold a below-floor value. It is also what made the first classifier wrong. The kernel reports an unreal component as a *format* error and a below-floor completion as a *date* error, and the constructor collapses both into one `none`, so the caller mapped the pair to whichever cause it guessed. Thirty-five of thirty-six measured cells agreed; the one that did not was the floor.

**The rule is about what an `Option`-returning constructor is allowed to decide.** A smart constructor may enforce any invariant it likes, but every rejection it folds into `none` becomes indistinguishable to its caller. When a caller must *classify* the failure — assign a diagnostic, choose a cause, explain a refusal — the constructor's `none` can carry at most one meaning. Either the caller tests the distinguishable conditions itself *before* constructing, or the constructor returns a reason rather than an option.

Two corollaries worth keeping.

- **Order the caller's own gates by the observable, not by convenience.** The measured order here is reality, then position in time, then the declaration's precision, and it is observable: an impossible month standing beside a legally omitted day reports the format cause, so the calendar question is settled before the omission question. Testing the floor first would have been equally natural to write and wrong in exactly one cell.
- **The retained cases must name the cause, not the refusal.** A row asserting "this text is rejected" passes under both accounts, because both reject it. Only rows asserting *which* cause distinguish them — the same point [`LF80`](#lf80--a-separator-that-both-accounts-satisfy-is-not-a-separator-and-a-boolean-lock-hides-which-one-you-have) makes about verdicts, reappearing in the diagnostic channel.

## LF102 — a consumer that accepts raw text already contains the classifier you are about to write, in a coarser form

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: §6. Basis: `checkSourceRaw` in [`ValueAsDate.lean`](../A12Kernel/Elaboration/ValueAsDate.lean) beside the classifier added in [`PartialDateInput.lean`](../A12Kernel/Elaboration/PartialDateInput.lean).

The partial-Date stored classifier was built as a new capability, on the strength of `ValueAsDate.lean`'s own module docstring saying that "detailed formal-error codes" remained separate. That sentence was true and still misleading: the module did not carry the *codes*, but it did carry the whole admission ladder — a lexical splitter, a suffix-legality test, calendar and floor tests, and the precision gate — inside a private raw adapter that mapped every one of those failures to a single `malformed` finding. So the new capsule reimplemented a decision that already existed, and the two copies disagreed on every rejection.

**The search that would have found it is not a search for the capability, it is a search for the consumer's input surface.** Grepping for the partial-value *type* found `ValueAsDate` immediately and looked like confirmation that only the value domain lived there. What was needed was the question "does any consumer already accept raw text for this kind?" — `RawCell String`, `checkRawCellWith`, a `parse`/`admit` private helper. A consumer that takes text must already decide admission, and a coarse cause is exactly how that decision hides: it produces no constructor a grep for the interesting codes would match.

Two consequences worth keeping.

- **A deliberate coarsening reads as an absence.** "All failures become the ordinary malformed finding" is a documented decision, not a gap, so it does not appear in any gap list and the next author sees no owner. When coarsening a cause on purpose, name the distinctions being collapsed, so the eventual refinement is a rewrite of one function rather than a second implementation of it.
- **Consolidating corrected a defect the duplication was hiding.** Routing the adapter through the single owner changed one retained case from `malformed` to the measured cause, and that case's own comment claimed to exercise unknown-year non-relevance while its precision made the value a rejection instead — so the row had never tested what it said. One owner made both errors visible at once.

## LF103 — a format string that appears in kernel source may belong to a different vocabulary, and copying it by symmetry certifies illegal models

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: §6. Basis: the [format-vocabulary checkpoint](SOURCES.md#src-temporal-format-vocabulary) at kernel `30.8.1`, `DateUtils` and `FieldTypeConstants` at a12-kernel `cb66e51fa7ab90b650698f861bf670754e2e1e66`, and the narrowing in [`TemporalTarget.lean`](../A12Kernel/Semantics/TemporalTarget.lean).

`DateTimeTargetFormat.ofSource?` admitted two declarable DateTime formats: the ISO `yyyy-MM-dd'T'HH:mm:ss` and the day-first `dd.MM.yyyy'T'HH:mm:ss`. Both strings are real kernel source. The model gate refuses the second one. The two live in different places and mean different things: the ISO string is the field-type constant's DateTime **default**, while the day-first string sits in the parser's date utilities and tags DateTime-valued **expressions** — the point-in-time operation, the `DateTime(...)` constructor, a DSL constant. It names no declarable format at all.

**The error was a symmetry, not a misreading.** Date genuinely has two declarable spellings, `yyyy-MM-dd` and `dd.MM.yyyy`, so a DateTime with two spellings looked like the obvious shape, and a grep for the day-first string found it in kernel source and confirmed the guess. What the grep could not show is which *vocabulary* the constant serves. A format string is not self-describing: its meaning is the set of positions that accept it, and that set is only visible from its callers.

Three consequences worth keeping.

- **A false acceptance in a legality vocabulary is quieter than a false refusal, and spreads.** Nothing failed. Seven conformance modules then built their DateTime fixtures on the unauthorable format, so the defect acquired 24 declaration sites and 40 rendered expectations before anything measured the gate. A refusal would have been noticed by the first fixture that needed it.
- **Migrating away from an illegal fixture can silently delete a discriminator.** One module used the day-first format as its *different-format* control; rewriting it to ISO made it identical to the source and the case's own `native_decide` caught that. The control now uses the legal degenerate clock format, authorable precisely because the format gate is kind-independent. A blanket fixture rewrite is not mechanical when the fixture's *difference* is the tested property.
- **Ask what a vocabulary excludes, not only what it contains.** The admitted set looked complete and self-consistent from the inside. The measurement that mattered was a refusal, and the cheapest version of it is one authoring call per candidate string.

## LF104 — before paying to enforce an invariant across a family, check whether one of the family's own clauses needs it violated

> Date: 2026-08-23, revised the same day after the second round measured it. Sections: §6. Basis: two attempts at a temporal arm in `canonicalScalarCoherent` in [`CheckedDocument.lean`](../A12Kernel/Elaboration/CheckedDocument.lean), the modules it broke, and the property that replaced it.

The checked document re-derives a stored `dateRange` cell from its text and requires the caller's `RawCell` to agree; Boolean, Confirm, and Number have the same arm. The four temporal kinds had none, so a cell could carry any raw beside any stored text. Adding the arm for the two kinds whose classifiers produce a `RawCell` broke ten conformance modules at once.

**The first reading was that all ten were fixture debt, and that reading was wrong.** Round one reverted the arm and recorded the estimate. Round two migrated the fixtures — deriving each target cell's value from its own stored label, which is a real improvement and was kept — and the remaining failures were the finding. `TemporalValuesNotUnique` supplies two cells whose stored text and decoded value **disagree on purpose**, because the duplicate-value clause compares the *exact stored text* and no coherent pair can separate that account from a decoded-value one. Its case already said so, and already recorded under `SPEC-2026-08-05-03` that whether a real model can store such a pair is open. `FullDateFirstFilled`'s `copied-text-would-be-wrong` cell and `DateTimeFirstFilled`'s instant-versus-wall-label separator are the same shape. A gate would have deleted the subject of a clause this project had already measured.

So the invariant became `CheckedDocument.temporallyCoherent`, a property a consumer asks for. Nothing broke, no claim was lost, and the deliberately incoherent documents are now *asserted* incoherent instead of merely tolerated.

Four consequences worth keeping.

- **Ask what the family's clauses observe before enforcing agreement between two of its fields.** A representation may only force `x = f(y)` if no clause's meaning is "this reads `x`, not `y`". Here one clause's whole content was which of the two it reads. This is the general form; the earlier version of this finding drew the opposite lesson — "a coherence gate belongs in a family's first capsule, before its fixtures exist" — which would have installed the wrong invariant *earlier* and made it harder to remove.
- **An invariant that can only cover part of a family is a property, not a gate.** Only a full-precision Date and a DateTime have an owning input classifier; a partial Date denotes an interval with no `Value` form and a Time carries no instant. A gate covering half a family is one no consumer can rely on, whereas a property can honestly report on the half it knows — and can *assert* the limit, which the six conformance cases now do.
- **A fixture parameter that looks dead may be read by the expected value rather than the builder.** Migrating `DateTimeDayShiftComputation` by underscoring its `targetStored` parameter compiled and left one case failing: two adjacent cases differ *only* in that label, because it is what decides whether a computed result counts as a change. The parameter had no reader in the builder and the entire discriminator in the case. Same class as [LF103](#lf103--a-format-string-that-appears-in-kernel-source-may-belong-to-a-different-vocabulary-and-copying-it-by-symmetry-certifies-illegal-models): a mechanical sweep over fixtures destroys controls, and only the case's own `native_decide` reports it.
- **Coherent fixtures are still worth having where nothing needs them violated.** A case that pairs unrelated stored text with a real raw tests a computation against a document that cannot exist. It is not wrong about the computation, but it is less separating than it looks, and the four migrated modules are better for the change even though no gate requires it.

## LF105 — a coverage estimate keyed on operator names undercounts this estate by a factor, because operators are modelled as enum cases inside their mechanism

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: all. Basis: an operator-by-operator audit of the kernel's own catalog (`dmtool operators`, 110 operators verified against 30.8.1) against `spec/` and the Lean tree.

Asked how much of the kernel is covered, the obvious method is to take the catalog as the denominator and grep each operator's keyword. That gave **71 of 110 in Lean, 64%**, and it is badly wrong. Four rounds of resolution took it to about **109 of 110**.

The reason is the estate's own design rule: laws are proved once at the owning mechanism and operators land as *cases*, so an operator's keyword usually appears nowhere. The five ordering comparisons are `StringLengthComparisonOp` and `NumericComparisonOp` cases; the four tolerance operators are one `NumericValidationOp.tolerance` arm; `RoundUp`/`RoundDown`/`RoundAccounting` are `DecimalRoundingMode.ceiling`/`.floor`/`.halfUp`; the value-list quantifiers are `ValueListQuantifier`; `PatternMatched`/`PatternViolated` are `StringPatternOp`; `ValidType`/`InvalidType` are `CustomFieldValidity`; `FromScope` is `RepetitionNotUnique`'s `@From` clause; `EnumCategory` is the category projection inside `FlatComparison.enumeration`; and `DifferenceInHours`/`Minutes`/`Seconds` are `DateTimeSubdayUnit` cases in `DateTimeDifference.lean`, whose `Int.tdiv` is exactly the spec's `floor(|Δ|/unit)` with the sign from operand order.

**Three of the four gaps I first reported did not exist.** That estimate reached the user before it was checked, which is the failure the discharge rule exists to prevent: a coverage number is a *reachability* claim about this repository, and reading a grep is not measuring it. The one real gap the audit did find, `NumberOfFilledFields`, was then worth a capsule.

Four things worth keeping.

- **Resolve a name-based miss in four passes before believing it.** Keyword, then lowerCamel enum case, then the operator's *catalog meaning* against concept words, then the owning mechanism's enum by reading it. Nine misses fell to pass two alone.
- **Read the catalog's `keyword` column, not its `id`.** Equality is `==`; a condition written with `=` is `MVK_UNEXPECTED_TOKEN`, and that cost a whole probe batch whose six rows all failed for one syntactic reason while looking like six semantic verdicts.
- **A missing *sibling* is the shape a real gap has.** `NumberOfFilledGroups` existed and `NumberOfFilledFields` did not; `TimeFromDateTime` existed and `DateFromDateTime` did not. Both were genuine, and both were found by asymmetry rather than by counting.
- **Report coverage in completion levels, not one number.** Representation ran near-complete while kernel correspondence was far thinner, and a single percentage hides exactly the layer that is actually the ceiling.

## LF106 — a probe channel that reports *changes* makes a suppressed operation and a no-op operation identical, and the fix is a stale value rather than a second route

> Date: 2026-08-23. Sections: §9. Basis: three `:adapter:kernelProbe` requests over one `NumberOfFilledFields` computation, at a12-dmkits `108d047b787f0e049dcd2f4902f24d7523347a00`.

The question was what a computation does when one operand is formally invalid. The artifact's `computations.outcomes` came back **empty** — no entry for the target at all — with the operand's error in `formalErrorsInOperands`. The natural reading is that the computation never ran and the whole pass was suppressed at plan level, which is what I first concluded, and it contradicted `spec/09`'s existing "CLEARED + poisons" row.

Both readings were wrong for the same reason. `outcomes` reports **changes**, and the target was already empty, so clearing it changed nothing and produced no entry. Re-running the identical row with a stale `9` in the target produced `{"field": "Order/Expected", "cleared": true, "errored": false}` on both strategies. The spec row was right all along and the observation was a no-op, not a suppression.

Three things worth keeping.

- **Before claiming an operation did not happen, check that its happening would have been visible.** A delta channel cannot distinguish "did nothing" from "did nothing *observable*". This is the same shape as the empty-substitution traps in the semantics itself, arriving through the instrument instead.
- **Give a clearing test a stale value, always.** One pre-filled target turns an unfalsifiable absence into a positive outcome record. It costs one extra row.
- **A contradiction with a settled `spec/` row is evidence against the reading, not the row.** The row had its own basis; a fresh observation that appears to refute it should first be suspected of measuring something else. Checking that direction cost one probe and prevented a wrong ledger entry against an already-correct clause.

It also answered the semantic question in the other direction: the field count needs **no** computation-arm rule, because its invalid operand *is* the operand and the general poison rule fires first. `NumberOfFilledGroups` needs two arms only because an invalid *descendant* leaves its group operand present. The asymmetry between the two counts is structural, not a special count behaviour.

## LF107 — a matrix that pins one axis across every row cannot see a second cause, and reads as complete while doing it

> Date: 2026-08-29. Sections: §11. Basis: four instances of the same defect within two days, in two repositories; the [declaring-group gate checkpoint](SOURCES.md#src-computation-declaring-group-gate) settles the mechanism.

`MVK_ERROR_FIELD_NOT_IN_RULEGROUP` has **two independent derivation sources**: a per-row operand of a repeatable declaring group, and the written field's own repeatable scope. Four separate accounts each generalized from a matrix that held one of those axes at a single value, and every one of them was internally consistent:

- a12-dmkits' `KF212` varied the operand exhaustively while holding the written field non-repeatable, and concluded "placement alone never refuses".
- This project's `spec/09` varied placement while holding the operand fixed, and concluded the mirror image — that the gate reads placement and not the operand.
- Accepted `SPEC-2026-08-27-01` recorded that a sibling-star computation "must be declared at its target's own parent", having moved the declaration without re-spelling its relative operand; the resulting refusal was the operand failing to resolve, under a *different* code.
- This project's own `CheckedAddressedRepeatableTarget` encoded that parenthood reading as an equality, which no case could falsify because no case declared at an ancestor.

**The tell is not disagreement between rows.** Every row of every one of those matrices reproduces. The tell is an axis that appears in the *claim* but never varies in the *evidence*, and a matrix has no way to signal that from inside: rows that all agree look like a law.

Lean treatment: before a matrix supports a universal, list the axes the claim quantifies over and confirm each one takes at least two values somewhere in the retained rows. An axis pinned across every row bounds the claim to that value and must appear in its statement. Where an axis is genuinely unreachable in the current fragment, say `no witness known as of <rev>` rather than dropping the qualifier.

- **Two causes reachable under one diagnostic code need a row per cause, not per code.** All four accounts had rows that refused with this code; none had a row that refused with it for the *other* reason. Grouping by observed code hides exactly the structure the claim is about.
- **A cause that changes the code is the cheapest separator available.** Re-spelling the operand turned `MVK_ERROR_FIELD_NOT_IN_RULEGROUP` into `MVK_INVALID_ENTITY`, which is what finally separated placement from operand resolution. Look for a neighbouring code before building a subtler discriminator.
- This is [`LF80`](#lf80--a-separator-that-both-accounts-satisfy-is-not-a-separator-and-a-boolean-lock-hides-which-one-you-have) and [`LF100`](#lf100--a-refusal-names-an-entity-not-the-gates-subject-only-an-admitted-row-that-varies-the-entity-says-which-one-the-gate-reads) one level up. Those ask whether a *row* separates two accounts; this asks whether the *matrix* spans the claim, which a row-local check cannot answer.

## LF108 — an operator enumeration by name misses every operator that has no name

> Date: 2026-08-29. Sections: §4, §11. Basis: one committed wrong claim, corrected the same day; the [assignment-scale reach checkpoint](SOURCES.md#src-unsuppressed-assignment-scale-reach) settles the enumeration.

Deciding whether an internal fault arm is reachable required knowing which operators can raise a computed value's fractional scale above its target's. The catalog was searched by operator id for division, multiplication, and their synonyms; nothing matched, and `SumOfProducts` was recorded as the only scale-increasing operator. That claim reached a keyed source record before `AddressedNumberDivision.lean` — a module this repository already owned — made it obvious that A12 has division.

The reason the search failed is structural, not careless. `*`, `/`, and `^` are **infix**, so they carry no catalog id resembling their meaning, and no name-based query reaches them however many synonyms it tries. An enumeration that only ever queried names cannot report that it never saw a whole syntactic class; it returns a clean, short, wrong list.

The conclusion happened to survive — all four operators are refused unsuppressed — but that was luck, not method. Had `*` been admitted where `SumOfProducts` was refused, the recorded claim would have been confidently false.

Lean treatment: before an enumeration over a catalog supports a claim, name the **syntactic classes** the catalog contains and confirm the query reaches each one. For A12 that is at least named functions, infix arithmetic, and comparison forms. Cross-check the result against this repository's own module list, which is an independent index of the same operator set and is what caught this instance.

- **A short answer from a catalog query is the warning sign, not the reassurance.** One hit for "every scale-increasing operator" should have prompted a second query shape rather than a record.
- This is [`LF107`](#lf107--a-matrix-that-pins-one-axis-across-every-row-cannot-see-a-second-cause-and-reads-as-complete-while-doing-it) in the enumeration domain. There the unvaried axis hid a second cause; here the unqueried syntactic class hid four more operators. Both fail the same way: the evidence looks complete from inside because everything it did collect agrees.

## LF109 — a representation limit justified by an unmeasured reachability claim is a defect with no failing test

`FlatModel` represents a group only through its fields or its own repeatable declaration, and `hasGroupPath` justified that with one sentence: *"Empty nonrepeatable groups are rejected by the kernel and therefore need no representation here."* Measured 2026-08-29, that is false. The Kernel admits an empty group, including a whole empty root, and reports the model valid ([checkpoint](SOURCES.md#src-empty-declared-group)).

Nothing failed. Sixteen gates ask `hasGroupPath`, every one of them is exact on the domain `FlatModel` can express, and no conformance case could ever have caught this, because the falsifying input is precisely the one the representation cannot build. The claim's own consequence is what hid it: having decided the shape was unreachable, the theory made it unrepresentable, and an unrepresentable shape produces no red case.

The claim then propagated. A gate written a day later split an undeclared root onto its own diagnostic; on a model with an empty root the Kernel reports the ordinary group class instead, so a new clause inherited a divergence from a sentence written long before it, silently and with every test green.

A sweep of the estate followed, over every kernel-side claim in semantics and elaboration source outside the conformance and proof trees. Six carried real weight. Three were properly backed — the year-class overlap gate, the `ValueAsDate` operand locus, and the group-operand wildcard gate all resolve to a measured checkpoint. Two were defective: this one, and a docstring asserting that a stored value unmapped by a category *cannot arise*, which was an unmeasured negative existential and is now stated as what the representation admits. One is backed but no longer re-dischargeable, because the CLI's own preflight has since closed the route. Two defects in six is the base rate worth planning around; internal unreachability claims about the project's own certificates were left aside, since those are discharged by the type checker rather than by the Kernel.

That exclusion was drawn too widely, found 2026-08-30 by a capsule rather than by another sweep. An internal claim can also be **universal over the estate** — `groupSubtreeFields` asserted that *every* group expansion in the theory routes through it — and a claim of that shape is not discharged by the type checker at all, because a site that filters the declaration list itself type-checks exactly as well. One site did, silently enumerating a narrower extent than the query it bypassed. The discharge for an estate-universal claim is a grep or a law, never a type; this one now has both.

The sibling claim was then discharged the same way rather than assumed. Group **reference** predicates are a second family — they test membership instead of enumerating an extent, so they do not route through `groupSubtreeFields` and the law above does not reach them. Enumerated 2026-08-30 over every `referencesField` definition under `Elaboration/`, six of which carry a group: the resolved group reference, the group-list operand and its starred arm, and the Number, token, and Boolean entity groups. All six test the whole subtree, four through an expansion the shared query built and two through a direct prefix test. No defect, which is the point worth recording — the enumeration is what makes that sentence safe to write, and without it this would be one more claim of the kind this finding is about.

Lean treatment: a reachability claim that **gates a representation** is the highest-cost class of unverified claim in this repository, because the representation it produces destroys the evidence that would refute it. Measure it before narrowing a type, or state the limit representationally — *"this type expresses only X"* — which is checkable from the definition and stays true whatever the Kernel does. Never write the Kernel-side version, which is a negative existential nothing local can discharge. This is [`LF75`](#lf75--writing-a-research-guard-and-applying-it-are-different-acts-the-same-commit-can-do-the-first-and-fail-the-second) and [`LF84`](#lf84--a-handbacks-claims-enter-spec-through-an-unguarded-door-and-the-discharge-table-was-written-only-for-claims-going-out) reaching the type layer rather than the prose layer, and it is worse there: prose gets re-read, a type's domain does not.

## LF110 — a two-signal attribution rule is only as strong as its weaker signal, and picking the wrong signal is the same mistake with more confidence

This project's rule for attributing a `dmtool` refusal to the Kernel names two signals at once: *"a kernel verdict carries `verification: KERNEL_CONFIRMED` and an `MVK_*` diagnostic"*. A conjunction is safe to apply and unsafe to reason about, because it never says which half carries the weight.

The first attempt at fixing that got the answer backwards. It concluded that the `MVK_*` code was the load-bearing half — *"an `MVK_*`-coded diagnostic can only come from a kernel notification"* — and shipped that into two owner documents and this finding. **It is false.** Measured at a12-dmkits `041346095` after the peer falsified it, `Diagnostic.precheck` and `precheckWarning` hard-code `DiagnosticSource.PRECHECK` and carry `MVK_*` codes: eleven of them, minted by rulekit's typed builders so an author sees the class the kernel would name. `Rule.deferredChecks()` is the sharpest, a **WARNING** riding a *successful* envelope, so a consumer scanning `diagnostics[]` on a green run meets an `MVK_*` code no kernel produced.

**The form that survives both measurements is the pair.** A kernel basis is `source: KERNEL` **and** an `MVK_*` code. Neither attributes alone: `source: KERNEL` also reaches a codeless deserialize-tier refusal, and an `MVK_*` code also rides `PRECHECK`. Only together do they exclude every producer but `fromKernel`.

The retained evidence survives, but on the **route** rather than on the code. Those builder pre-checks fire only on a typed object, and the DSL-bearing CLI authoring verbs pass a draft; `patterns` names itself in its own source as the unique verb that instantiates the typed form, and this project does not use it. That is a different argument from the one this finding first gave, and it is the one that is true.

Two lessons, and the second is the one that cost the round. **When a rule names several signals, ask which of them a wrong implementation could still emit** — but answer it by enumerating producers, not by checking whether the signal *looks* authoritative. And **a negative existential over someone else's estate is a scope claim** under the discharge table: "no source mints such a literal" needed a complete enumeration, and got a truncated one ([`LF112`](#lf112--a-command-whose-output-settles-a-claim-is-an-observation-even-when-it-is-only-a-grep)).

The peer reached the same conclusion by a different route and got its own enumeration wrong in the same direction, asserting that `source: KERNEL` is produced *only* by the kernel-notification constructor; two further producers exist, both codeless, so its conclusion survived and its enumeration did not. Two projects, one day, the same failure on the same class of claim. An enumeration of producers needs measurement even when the enumerator owns the code — and especially when it does not.

## LF111 — an obligation's stated reason is an unverified claim too, and a wrong one parks work indefinitely

[`SEMANTICS-GAPS.md`](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md) carried the message group position's three lexical refusal codes as open, with a reason attached: *"which this fragment reports as its one parse class **because it tracks no character position**"*. The reason was wrong, and it was wrong in the direction that keeps work parked — it named a hard prerequisite the item did not actually have.

The Kernel's three classes here are an unbalanced quote, a trailing separator, and a doubled separator. Only the first of those reports a character position at all, and it reports it as a *payload* beside the class. The class itself follows the malformed **shape**: a quote fault, an empty final segment, an empty non-final segment. The existing decoder already walked the segments, so the position that decides — which segment failed and whether it was last — was in hand at the point of decision and simply discarded. Closing it was one `Except`-returning helper and a four-row separator matrix, against a reason that read like it needed a lexer rewrite.

Every claim leaving this repository must be discharged by its class, and a gap's `remaining` line is a claim about the world: *this cannot be done without X*. That is a reachability claim, whose discharge is measurement, and nothing measured it. Worse, it is self-concealing in the way [`LF109`](#lf109--a-representation-limit-justified-by-an-unmeasured-reachability-claim-is-a-defect-with-no-failing-test) describes: an item nobody selects is an item whose justification nobody re-reads, and the justification is why nobody selects it.

Two further instances landed the same way, both in [SG4](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md), and together they show the failure has a second mode. One carried a *wrong reason*, like the case above: the starred group-count operand was deferred for needing a row-topology channel and a second consumer to shape it, when the addressed evaluator already carried the topology and the operand representation already had two users — the whole obligation was one surface type away. The other carried a *stale status*: addressed Custom and ordinary String were recorded as separating placement "only against a wholly unrelated group", while both conformance modules already held the sharp three-way separator — unrelated refused, ancestor admitted, prefix-sharing-but-not-containing refused. Nothing was wrong when either line was written; the work that closed one and the reading that refuted the other both landed under a different family's capsule, and neither came back to the gap.

So a `remaining` line decays in two directions, and both are invisible from the line itself: its *reason* can be false, and its *status* can be obsolete. The second is the cheaper to check and the easier to miss, because a stale line reads exactly like a live one.

A third mode is worse than either, because it is not decay at all — the line was wrong when written. An [SG13](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md#sg13--group-list-and-group-count-completion) record read *still unmeasured on this carrier and **therefore** refused or unrepresented*, and named four shapes. Three of them elaborated and answered; only the fourth refused, and for a reason belonging to the probe's unbound outer environment rather than to the representation. The defect is the word *therefore*: it derives an **implementation** status from an **evidence** status, and the governing instructions keep those as independent completion levels precisely because neither implies the other. An internally closed clause may sit `external evidence pending` indefinitely, which is exactly the state all three shapes were in.

The inference is attractive because it is usually true in the *other* direction — work that is unmeasured is often also unwritten — and it costs nothing to write, so it slips in as a summary rather than as a claim. Its cost is that it hides finished work behind a gap: the correction converted three items from "needs a representation decision" to "needs one calibration row", which is a different and much smaller job. Checking it is four `#eval`s against the authored surface. So when a gap line joins two completion levels with *therefore*, *and so*, or *hence*, treat the join itself as the claim and run the shapes; and never write a status line that derives one level from another, since stating each independently is the same number of words. The detector was then run over the gap file and all seven implementation-map documents: eighteen lines pair an evidence word with an implementation word, all eighteen read, and every one besides the corrected record states the two levels independently or derives non-executability from a **measured** refusal, which is sound. So this was an isolated instance rather than a habit — recorded with its denominator, because "I checked and found nothing else" without one is the negative existential this project keeps getting wrong.

Treatment: when selecting from an open obligation, read its stated blocker as a hypothesis and spend the two minutes to check it against the code before believing it — especially when it names a missing capability rather than a missing observation. A missing *observation* is checkable against the evidence routes; a missing *capability* is a claim about this estate, and this estate is right here. Write the reason so it can be checked: name the exact declaration or gate that would have to change, not a paraphrase of why it feels hard.

## LF112 — a command whose output settles a claim is an observation, even when it is only a grep

[`TESTING.md`](TESTING.md#structured-dmtool-probes-and-feedback) already carried the rule: *"Never pipe a probe's output through `head`, `tail`, `cut`, or any other viewport command when that output is the observation"*, with the reason spelled out — a truncated result is indistinguishable from a complete one, and `head -N` returning exactly N lines is not evidence that N was the end. It even cited the incident that produced it: a defect report filed on an 11-line output read through `head -10`, which cost the peer a full measurement round.

It happened again the same session, and the rule did not stop it, because the rule says **probe** and this was a `grep`. Checking whether any a12-dmkits source mints an `MVK_*` literal into a diagnostic, the search matched **488** occurrences and was read through `head -20`. Four percent of the evidence, and the missing 96% contained forty-eight files in the `dsl/` package that mint eleven such codes. The conclusion drawn from those twenty lines — *an `MVK_*`-coded diagnostic can only come from a kernel notification* — reached two owner documents and [`LF110`](#lf110--a-two-signal-attribution-rule-is-only-as-strong-as-its-weaker-signal-and-picking-the-wrong-signal-is-the-same-mistake-with-more-confidence) before the peer falsified it.

The scoping error is the whole finding. "Probe" reads as the deliberate, ceremonial thing — a `dmtool` invocation, a kernel run, something with a revision to record. A `grep` over a sibling checkout feels like looking around. But it was the sole evidence for a negative existential, which is the claim class least tolerant of a partial view: one unseen line refutes it, and truncation guarantees unseen lines. The ceremony is irrelevant; what matters is whether the output is load-bearing.

A negative existential also inverts the usual reading of a short result. A grep that returns *few* hits feels like a clean answer, and under `head` it looks identical whether the corpus held twenty matches or five hundred. The tell that would have caught this — piping to `wc -l` first — costs one command and is the same command that sizes the read.

Treatment: widen the rule from *a probe's output* to **any command whose output settles a claim**, and count the matches before reading them. When the claim is that something does *not* exist, enumerate exhaustively or state the search's exact scope and matched count alongside it, so a reader can see what the claim rests on. `LF107`'s unvaried axis, `LF110`'s wrong conjunct, and this truncated corpus are one family: evidence that looks complete from inside because everything it did collect agrees.

## LF113 — prose about this project's own mechanism is a claim too, and it is the one whose refutation is already in the repository

The repeatable-descendant measurement was written up as *"the notion of content changes rather than extending"* — a row rule replacing the cell rule. The peer disputed it and was right, but the decisive evidence was never on their side of the boundary. `ResolvedGroupPresenceInput.derive` reads:

```
content := input.hasInstantiatedRow || input.descendantCells.any CheckedCell.admitsGroupContent
```

A plain disjunction of two constituents, and it had said that for as long as the query existed. In the same session it was locked with a conformance case whose own comment says the two sources agree where both can fire. Then the prose claimed one replaced the other.

`spec/` had said it too, four paragraphs above the sentence that contradicted it. The clause on a non-repeatable terminal below starred ancestors already read: *"Instantiating an ancestor row does not by itself fill `Details`; **admitted scalar content or an instantiated repeatable descendant** does."* Same disjunction, same file, stated for a neighbouring operand shape — so the new clause was inconsistent with its own document before it was inconsistent with anything external.

Three of the four wrong claims made that day were about a12-dmkits' estate, where checking costs a search and truncation or a missing measurement can defeat it. This one was about a definition in this repository, twelve lines from the case that exercised it and four paragraphs from the prose that stated it, and checking it cost a single `grep`. It is the cheapest claim class in the project and the one that got the least verification, for the obvious reason: nobody re-reads what they believe they already know.

There is no gate for it. The trust audit checks axioms, the source-hygiene gate checks shape, and the doc-hygiene gate checks size and ownership; none compares a sentence about a mechanism with the mechanism. A conformance case cannot fail because a document describes it wrongly, and a wrong description is exactly as portable as a right one — this one reached `spec/`, a ledger entry, two Lean docstrings, and a peer, inside an hour.

Treatment: when a claim's subject is a declaration in this repository, **read the declaration in the same edit that describes it**, and prefer quoting its shape over paraphrasing its behaviour. A paraphrase drifts; `a || b` does not. Two tells were available and both were missed: the description said *replaces* where the retained case beside it said *agree*, and it introduced a rule for one operand shape that a nearby clause already stated for its neighbour. **Before adding a rule to a `spec/` section, grep that section for the rule it is about** — a family's clauses are written years apart and the earlier one is usually right, because it was measured when the family was the subject rather than the aside. This is [`LF112`](#lf112--a-command-whose-output-settles-a-claim-is-an-observation-even-when-it-is-only-a-grep) with the corpus at zero distance: there the evidence was truncated, here it was never opened.

## LF114 — both sides sampling one side of an unnamed boundary agree, and re-derivation cannot break the tie

Four claims were caught in one day by the other project re-deriving them, so re-derivation looked like the method that works. Then it failed. Measuring the computation-arm message polarity, this project ran a single-run witness — one document, four computations, a wildcard-free control — and concluded that a starred operand types `OMISSION` even with every referenced cell filled. a12-dmkits independently measured the same split on its own model, from its own field-count fixtures, and agreed. Two projects, two models, two engines, one conclusion, and it was false: at **declared capacity** the starred targets type `VALUE`, and one document at capacity with empty rows splits a starred count from a starred `Sum` over the same group.

Nothing about the process failed. Every row on both sides was correctly measured and correctly reported. The rows were all taken **below capacity**, where the false account fits perfectly, and neither side had thought to name capacity as an axis — so neither sampled the other side of it. Re-derivation is a check on whether a claim follows from the evidence, and here it did on both sides; it cannot detect that the evidence lies entirely within one region. The peer's independent agreement made the account look better confirmed while adding no new region.

The move that caught it was different in kind: asking **what would have to be true for the account to be false**, and building that document. "A starred operand always types OMISSION" is false if a starred extent can ever stop growing — and a declared `max` is exactly that, sitting in the model all along. That question is answerable from the account's own wording, before any measurement, and it costs one probe.

a12-dmkits stated the day's result more weakly and more accurately than the paragraphs above, and its version is the one to keep: the two-implementation setup did not make either project more careful, it made carelessness **recoverable**. Across thirteen wrong claims from the two sides, not one was caught by its author re-reading their own prose. Every correction came from the other project re-deriving, from a definition already sitting in one of the two repositories, or — twice — from an unrelated failure surfacing something incidentally, such as an edit aborting on an anchor that a rewrite had silently dropped.

Two claims were caught by their own author, and both by the same means. This project's `spec/10` polarity bullet was written, guard-checked, and then falsified before commit by *executing a discriminator already recorded as the open question*; a12-dmkits' claim about its own catalog was falsified by running the binary and finding a stale launcher. Neither author noticed while writing. So the distinction is not author-versus-peer but **review-versus-execution**: across thirteen wrong claims from two projects, re-reading caught zero and running something caught two.

It happened a second time on the same operator, which is what makes the shape worth naming rather than the instance. Having fixed the capacity boundary, both projects then held the message-polarity account as "OMISSION when the quantity can still grow" — and every row on both estates had its computed value **below** its stored seed, so headroom and direction moved together and neither conjunct was tested alone. One document with the seed *below* the computed value refutes the coarse reading: the count still has headroom and types VALUE, because growing moves it away. Same operator, same failure shape, one boundary later. The tell is identical both times and is available without measuring: an account states one condition, and every row held varies that condition **together with** something else nobody has named.

A third instance moved the constant out of the data and into the **fixture**. This project claimed a message's referenced set names its operand group's whole subtree; every group in every model here owns exactly one field, so *names its subtree* and *names the one field it has* are the same sentence on every row, and no document could separate them. The peer's fixture gives its groups two fields for an unrelated reason and separated them by accident. So the unnamed second variable need not be document state at all: it can be a structural property of every model the claim was measured on, which no amount of varying the data will expose. The question that covers all three is asked of the model as well as the rows — *what do all my fixtures have in common that a reader of my claim would not assume?*

A fourth instance arrived with the falsifier already written down. This project measured the over-limit finding multiplicity, wrote the rule into `spec/` and a pending peer entry, and recorded in the same change a `limit` line naming the shapes it had not measured — among them *an over-limit row beneath another over-limit row*. That list was not a caveat. It was the falsifier list, assembled at exactly the moment its author could see which shapes the rule left under-determined, and then shipped instead of run. Running it cost one request and corrected the rule twice within the hour: the stamped node is the instantiated **row** and not the group key, which only a descendant level holding more than one row can show; and a nested over-limit row is **absorbed**, drawing no code of its own while every message beneath it names the ancestor's group. So the tell has a cheaper form than *what do my fixtures have in common* — the residuals about to be written into a `limit` line are the shapes most likely to falsify the claim in the line above it. Measure them before the entry ships, or state no rule beyond what they leave determined.

A fifth instance had its defeater sitting in this repository's own `spec/`, one clause away, in prose this project wrote. Having measured that a Number constant of `1.5` stores `1.5` in a two-digit target, this project concluded *the authored scale is retained* and began writing that into a Lean carrier. `spec/09` already stated the general rule for computed Number output — strip trailing zeros, then render at `max(naturalScale, minFractionalDigits)`, with `10` into a two-decimal field storing `10.00` — and a bare literal turns out to take exactly that path. Every fixture held `minFractionalDigits: 0` and no constant was written with a trailing zero, so *retained*, *stripped*, and *padded* were the same sentence on every row. Three probe rows separated them and the conclusion was false in both directions at once. So the check has a form cheaper still than measuring: **before writing a clause about a special case, read what `spec/` already says about the general case it instantiates.** The existing instruction to grep `spec/` for a defeater is scoped to negative existentials; this one was a positive claim, and the defeater was a positive clause describing the mechanism the claim's subject is an instance of. Same file, same grep, one clause away, and it cost a measurement round to find from the other end.

That is not a new rule and must not be recorded as one. The governing instructions already say it — *define success criteria, loop until an oracle or a guard verifies them, not until re-reading convinces you* — and a second live statement of a fact its owner already carries is what this project's own consolidation rule forbids. Today is thirteen data points of **non-compliance** with a rule both projects already held, which is a less flattering finding than discovery and the accurate one. The peer declined to codify it for exactly this reason, and that judgment is recorded here rather than acted on twice.

Treatment: for any claim of the form *X always does Y*, name the mechanism the account depends on, ask what would exhaust or disable it, and measure that point specifically before recording the claim. Peer agreement raises confidence in a *reading* of shared evidence, never in the evidence's coverage; when both sides sampled the same region, two confirmations are one. And a hedge is not a substitute — `LF113`'s prose overshoot and this one were both labelled as interpretations rather than measurements, which read as caution and bought nothing, because the interpretation was the part that was wrong and it was stated as the claim's own title. Hedge the confidence, then go measure the boundary anyway.

## LF115 — the peer-message channel carries claims into another project's records and passes through no gate
