Everything else that leaves this repository is gated. A `spec/` change meets the synchronization guard; a ledger entry meets the doc-hygiene guard and a structured record contract; Lean meets the build, the trust audit, and the conformance cases; a finding meets a reviewer eventually. A message to the peer session meets nothing — and it is the surface the peer actually reads, quotes, and cites into its own findings record.

The day that produced this finding produced seven wrong claims from this side, and **three of them existed only in messages**: that a witness ruled out an account it was in fact consistent with; that `dmtool operators` could not serve a fact it can; and a correction ratio stated without its denominator, twice, the second time hours after committing to state denominators first. None could have been caught here, because none was in a file. Two were caught by the peer; the third only surfaced when an edit to fix something else failed on a missing anchor.

The gap is visible in the governing rule's own text. [`CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md)'s discharge rule opens by naming the cost — a false statement "enters `spec/`, **ships to the peer**, and costs a reconciliation round" — and then enumerates the channels it governs as "`spec/`, a ledger entry, a docstring, a finding, or a reply to the user." The rationale names the peer channel; the enumeration omits it. That is not a drafting slip to route around silently: peer sessions were not a channel when it was written, and whether to widen it is the owner's call, so it is raised rather than edited.

Settled: the owner widened the discharge rule's channel list to name a peer-session message, and [`TESTING.md`](TESTING.md#peer-session-coordination) now owns the coordination protocol. The operative discipline is unchanged and was always available: **a claim in a peer message is a claim.** Before asserting a capability, a limit, or a ratio in one, apply the discharge table to it exactly as if it were going into `spec/` — a capability claim about the peer's own tool is a *reachability* claim and needs a measurement, not a reading of the source that feeds it, and a ratio is a *value* claim that is meaningless until its denominator is stated. Prefer sending what a record already says over composing a fresh account in prose, because the record was gated and the message will not be. When a rewrite drops a sentence, the dropped sentence may be the one a message already shipped; the record and the message can disagree, and only the record gets re-read.

## LF116 — reusing a mechanism inherits its evidence's scope, not the mechanism's generality

Calling an existing function on a new carrier looks like reuse and is often a claim. The function compiles wherever its types fit; what was *measured* about it is almost always narrower than that, and nothing in the code marks the edge. This is the rare defect with no tell — a new branch prompts the question "is this right?", and reusing a settled mechanism prompts nothing at all, because the diff shows an ordinary call.

It has happened three times here. `NumberOfFilledGroups`' erroneous-operand rule was measured for a **direct** child and applied to a nested descendant; the checkpoint had to record that the extension was "uniformity rather than observation" until a capture replaced it. Then `CheckedStarredGroupSource.inCapacityRowCount` was reused for the compute arm's starred count, carrying an over-limit exclusion whose only evidence was on the **validation** arm — while the two arms are known to diverge on this very operator, for a formally invalid operand. Both assumptions turned out to be **correct**, which is exactly what makes them dangerous: a silent assumption that holds produces no signal, teaches nothing, and leaves the next one unexamined.

A third instance widened the boundary from carriers to **siblings a search grouped together**. A grep named three visitor files under the kernel's mandatory-information package; one was read, its `z == -1` bail-out was understood, and the ledger entry that left this repository said *the three visitors* branch on it. Two of them do not — they guard `z > 0`, which swallows the sentinel and every legitimate non-positive constant alike, so the corrective differs per file and the observable surface is one arm rather than three. The peer enumerated all six call sites and read each. The lesson generalizes the rule rather than adding a case: a search result is a list of *candidates*, and attributing one member's mechanism to the rest is the same act as carrying a measurement across an arm — it costs one read per site to discharge and produces a confident false enumeration when skipped.

The detector for the sibling case is narrower than the lesson and worth stating separately: **write the read fraction down**. The peer caught this project's false enumeration not by being careful but because its own record format requires the count — writing *six sites in three files, all six read* is what made it open the two files it had not. A check that forces an explicit denominator fires on the way past; a lesson about unread siblings has to be remembered at the moment it is being violated, which is exactly when it is not.

Treatment. When a mechanism is reused across an arm, carrier, phase, depth, or topology boundary, name the boundary explicitly and ask whether the two sides are already known to differ **anywhere**. A known divergence is proof the boundary is real, so a rule crossing it is a fresh claim under the discharge table and not an inheritance. Then choose visibly, never silently: cite the measurement that actually covers the new carrier, or state the assumption in the owning record and open the evidence item beside it. The second option is cheap and honest, and it is what makes the difference recoverable when the assumption is wrong. The cost of the check is one sentence; the cost of skipping it is a clause whose evidence does not reach where the clause applies, which is indistinguishable from a measured one when read later.

## LF117 — a semantic widening reaches only the dispatch arm its motivating form selects

The compute-arm group count has two checked atom shapes. A list carrying a star elaborates to `filledGroupCountMixed`; an all-fixed list elaborates to the plain `filledGroupCount`. Both reach the same addressed evaluator, and both hold the same `fixed` operand form.

Widening the fixed operand to count a repeatable-descendant shell was measured, red/green tested, proved, and landed — in the mixed atom only, because the starred operand was what motivated the work and the mixed atom is what a starred operand produces. The plain atom kept delegating to the scalar reader, which must refuse that shape. The result is the shape of defect a reviewer does not look for: `NumberOfFilledGroups(Shell, Preferences, Rows*)` counted `Shell`, and `NumberOfFilledGroups(Shell, Preferences)` refused the identical operand over the identical document. **One operand's admissibility became a function of a sibling operand's form**, and nothing in either arm was locally wrong.

Neither existing habit catches it. The conformance cases for the widening were written against the atom the new form produces, which is the honest thing to do and covers one arm completely. The `route` note in [`SEMANTICS-GAPS.md`](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md#sg13--group-list-and-group-count-completion) had even named the exact mechanism — *it delegates the group count to `scalar.readFilledGroupCount`, discarding all of it* — but named it while diagnosing the starred case, so it read as background rather than as a second site.

The guard that should have fired was worse than absent: `numericComputationEvaluationContext_filledGroupCount_delegates`, since replaced and no longer a declaration, stated by `rfl` that the addressed context resolves a fixed group count through the scalar reader and *"cannot silently acquire"* a document. It read as a safety property and was a transcription of the implementation, so it could not fail when the meaning changed underneath it — it failed only when the code was finally corrected, which is the opposite of a guard. A `rfl` theorem over one's own dispatch asserts nothing about meaning; the version that survives states what the count is **invariant in** (`outer`, `filterRead`, `starRead`) rather than what it delegates to.

The repair predicted a second instance, and there was one. Fixing the reader left the operand still refused end to end, because a **pre-read structural fault pass** decides admission separately and had its own narrower copy of the test — and the conformance case written for the repair called the reader directly, so it went green over a route that still refused. A case that exercises the mechanism is not the same as a case that exercises the route, and only the second kind can see a gate.

A third instance followed from the same enumeration, and it is the one that shows why the class is worth a finding rather than two fixes. The pre-read pass exists **because** a structural fault must not be hidden by data — `evalOrdered` stops at a left poison without reaching the right operand — and the mixed atom had no arm in that pass at all. So `poison + NumberOfFilledGroups(BadShell, X*)` returned poison and swallowed the operand's structural fault outright, while the plain atom over the identical operand reported it. The arm's own comment stated the hazard it was protecting against; the atom added beside it inherited the comment's neighbourhood and not its protection.

The pass's remaining arms were then read to a denominator rather than spot-checked: of its ten arms, the group count is the only one whose refusal is model-structural and therefore pre-decidable at all. `firstFilled`, both value counts, `sumOfProducts`, and a starred group operand all fail with addressing errors over the document's own row topology, so their `none` is correct rather than a fourth hole; and `dateTimeDifference`'s wildcard rejects exactly the constructors its reader rejects, with the gate stricter than the reader only on the kind check it exists for. Enumerating the predicate's callers then settled the other half: three sites test whether a group operand is admitted, two of them must share one predicate, and the third must **not**. `supportsScalarEvaluation` asks whether the scalar route can evaluate the atom, and that route reads cells only, so a repeatable descendant is genuinely outside it. The discriminator is which route a site describes, not which predicate is newer — so the enumeration's output is a partition, not a sweep, and the deliberate disagreement needs its own case or the next reader completes the sweep and breaks it.

The structural remedy already exists in this repository and is worth naming, because it makes the class unrepresentable rather than merely tested. `CheckedDateRangeEntityGroup` and `CheckedBooleanValueCountSource` carry their admission as **proof fields**, so the check and its consumer are one object and no second site can hold a narrower copy — a certificate cannot be built without discharging the condition it asserts. The group count is exposed precisely because `ResolvedGroupReference` carries no such certificate: it is a resolved path, admitted by whatever asks. Retrofitting one is a real change with real cost and is not proposed here; the point is that the two laws are the cheap substitute for a certificate, and where a family is starting fresh the certificate is the thing to reach for.

Treatment. When widening what an operand *means*, enumerate every dispatch arm and every admission site that can carry that operand form, rather than the arms the motivating example reaches — the enumeration is a grep for the operand's constructor and for the predicate the old narrow test used, not a reading of the new code. Check the widening through the **whole route** at least once, since a gate upstream of the reader is invisible to a reader-level case. Then state the agreement as laws over the sites (`readCheckedNumericComputationAtom_filledGroupCount_eq_mixed`, `numericComputationFault_filledGroupCount_eq_mixed`, `readGroupCountOperand_fixed_error_iff_notAdmitted`), because a law quantified over both is what makes the next widening fail loudly in the site it forgets. This is [`LF116`](#lf116--reusing-a-mechanism-inherits-its-evidences-scope-not-the-mechanisms-generality) one level down: there a mechanism inherited its evidence's scope, here a widening inherited its motivating form's **dispatch** scope.

## LF118 — an expected value derived from the implementation is not a lock, and `native_decide` makes it look like one

A conformance case asserted `numberOfFilledGroups [admittedAndErroneous, cleanFilled] = .unknown`, under the comment "the plain numeric count is stricter than the list tally". It was green, it was exhaustive over its fixture, it had survived every refactor — and it was wrong. The kernel counts that pair as 2. The case had never been measured: its expected value was read off the clause it was checking, so what it locked was that the implementation still did what the implementation did.

The tell is visible without the measurement, and it is a *local* one. Two lines above, sibling cases over the same fixture carry `[checkpoint](…)` links; this one carries a design sentence instead. In a repository where measured cases cite their rows, a case whose justification is a claim about the design is announcing its own provenance. The neighbouring cases were the control that made it legible, which is the practical version of the rule: read a conformance module's citations as a column, not case by case, and the uncited ones stand out.

What made the error survive was that the same fixture answered two questions with one name. `admittedAndErroneous` carries admitted content **and** a finding, so it separates the two dimensions perfectly — the fixture was not the problem. The problem is that nothing else in the module ever asked whether *content decides an erroneous group*, so the fixture's discriminating power was spent on an expectation nobody had checked. A fixture that separates a boundary and an expectation that was never measured against it combine into a case that looks maximally strong and is worth nothing. This is the failure [`LF114`](#lf114--both-sides-sampling-one-side-of-an-unnamed-boundary-agree-and-re-derivation-cannot-break-the-tie) describes with the roles swapped: there both samples sat on one side of a boundary nobody had named; here the samples straddle the boundary and the *oracle* is the thing that was never independently obtained.

The mechanism behind the wrong expectation is worth naming separately, because it is what a reviewer can catch. `GroupPresenceState` already had one decided-presence projection, `asGroupListPresence`, built from `definitelyFilled`/`definitelyEmpty` and used by every group-list predicate. `numberOfFilledGroups` did not use it: it re-derived an unavailability condition of its own, `erroneous || !fullyRelevant`. Two clauses over one state, one of them measured and the other guessed, sitting in the same file and disagreeing about the same input — the kernel fires `GroupFilled` on a group whose content is admitted beside an error, and this project's count called that same group unknown. A second copy of a condition does not merely risk drift; here it had already drifted before it was ever committed, and the count's own case froze the drift in place.

Treatment. When a clause computes an availability, unknown, or admission condition, grep the owning module for an existing projection over the same state before writing a new one, and define through it — the disagreement then cannot be expressed. When writing a conformance case, obtain the expected value from a row, a probe, or an independent derivation *before* looking at what the clause returns; if none is available, say so in the case rather than letting green stand in for measured. And when a measurement corrects a clause, re-read the laws over it: `erroneousHead_makesFilledGroupCountUnknown` was a theorem whose hypothesis had been chosen to match the defect, so it proved cleanly and pointed at nothing. Its replacements state the hypothesis that is actually true (`undecidedHead_…`) and the asymmetry that was measured (`filledGroupCount_indifferentToErrorOnDecidedGroup`), which is the pair that would have failed to elaborate against the old clause.

## LF119 — a conditional failure law with no witness is a hypothesis about the code, not a property of it

Five capability records carried the same open item in the same words: *no valid current fixture witnesses a structural failure, so failure reachability remains unclaimed.* Each had a conditional soundness law — *if a fault is returned, it decomposes into an exact successful prefix plus one enabled failure* — and each recorded the missing witness as an external-evidence gap, as though the Kernel were the thing that could settle it.

It could be settled locally, and the answer inverted the framing. A valid checked document does reach one of those arms: instantiate one target row beyond the group's declared capacity, and the indexed-parallel executor aborts the entire run with a structural key-lookup fault. The document passes every checker. Nothing about the Kernel is needed to find this — only the question *what input would make this branch fire*, asked of the branch rather than of the oracle.

And the witness was a **defect**. The index column skips over-limit rows while the target inventory enumerated every physical row, so the two disagreed on exactly one row and the executor aborted rather than skipping it. The Kernel computes into in-capacity rows only, so the correct behaviour was to exclude the row, and the arm's one reachable route closed with the fix. The conditional law had been true and vacuous, and its vacuity was the finding: the branch existed because two enumerations of one domain had drifted apart, not because a failure mode was being modelled.

That is the general shape. An unwitnessed failure branch is a claim that some input reaches it, and the claim has three possible answers — reachable and correct, reachable and a defect, or unreachable — which are not equally likely and are not equally cheap to distinguish. Recording it as *pending external evidence* asserts the first while checking none of them, and it is the answer the other two get filed under. The cost of leaving it there compounds: the same sentence propagated verbatim into five records, so one unasked question read as five independent gaps.

Treatment. When a failure arm has no witness, **derive one from the throw site before recording the gap**: read what the throw requires, construct the smallest input satisfying it, and run it. If it fires, decide whether the behaviour is right before writing a case for it — a witness for a defect looks exactly like a witness for a feature. If it cannot fire, say *unreachable from a checked input* with the argument, which is a stronger statement than the gap it replaces and needs no oracle. Reserve the external-evidence gap for the case where the arm fires and the **Kernel's** answer is what is unknown. Two enumerations of the same domain that agree everywhere except one edge are the specific smell here: the disagreement is invisible in every fixture that stays inside the common part, which is every fixture anyone writes on purpose.

## LF120 — the claim class follows the logical form, not the subject; a negative claim about an instrument is still reachability

> Date: 2026-08-31. Sections: §5 and §9. Basis: `A12-DMTOOL-FEEDBACK-2026-08-31.md` §1, retracted by note `-02` the same day; the [Base Year store checkpoint](sources/cross-layer-routes.md#src-base-year-yearless-store).

This project's claim-discharge table says reachability is **measurement only** and that reading never discharges it, and it bans the phrasings that hide an undischarged one. That discipline was applied carefully to every kernel claim in the session that produced this finding. Then the same session wrote, about `dmtool`, *"No verb sets it. `model new` has no option, no `model` subcommand modifies it, and it is not a `group add` or `field add` property."* The setter is `dmtool config modify --base-year`, documented in that subcommand's own `--help`, and it had existed all along.

**The claim's form was identical to the ones being guarded — a negative existential over a surface — and the discipline did not attach because the subject was the instrument rather than the object of study.** A tool feels like infrastructure, so a statement about it feels like a note rather than a claim. It is not: it left the repository in a feedback note, a peer quoted it into its own record, and that peer escalated a nonexistent CLI feature to its owner for a decision. Three local documents and a ledger entry additionally recorded a capture as "not reproducible from retained bytes" on its strength.

The search that produced it enumerated two surfaces and generalized over all of them. `model` subcommands and the `field add` JSON schema were both checked, and `config` was never enumerated at all — its options are CLI flags, so no amount of schema grepping could have found it. That is the recurring shape of a false negative: the denominator of the search is not the denominator of the claim, and nothing in the search reports the difference.

**The claim then survived a second party, which is the part that makes it expensive rather than merely wrong.** The peer holding the note read it, read the neighbouring CLI source, and forwarded it to its owner as confirmed without running the one command that refutes it — its own account of the failure was that the claim became its claim on arrival, because a citation reads stronger than the message behind it. Neither party needed the other's mistake. The discharge table already says *the peer said so is not discharge*; the symmetric half is that **a claim you send is one the receiver is unlikely to test**, so the cost of an undischarged negative does not stop at your own records. That is the concrete reason the peer-message rule prefers a pointer to a gated record over fresh prose: a note is prose, and prose travels further than its evidence.

Treatment. Before writing that a tool cannot do something, enumerate its subcommands and read the `--help` of every one whose name could plausibly own the setting — the cost is seconds and it is the only thing that discharges the claim. Absent that, write what was actually searched: *"not found under `model` or in the `field add` schema"* names a denominator and invites the correction, where *"no verb sets it"* does not. The same rule that forbids **unauthorable** for a kernel behavior forbids **no verb sets it** for a CLI, for exactly the same reason.

## LF121 — retained evidence is indexed by the question that motivated it, so the settler for a new question hides under an old row's label

> Date: 2026-08-31. Sections: §5 and §9. Basis: the [component-omitting format checkpoint](sources/temporal-and-message-probes.md#src-omitting-date-formats)'s `yearless-claim` and `order-claim` rows; a12-dmkits `b54cb52d0` drew the same inference independently.

A capsule shipped with a stated assumption: the two compact yearless date spellings, `MMdd` and `ddMM`, render in the component order the stored-input classifier reads them in. The assumption was carefully hedged, guarded by an exhaustive round-trip case, flagged in the capability record and the gap, and reported to the peer as open. A kernel probe then settled the writing half, and the peer settled the reading half from its own estate.

**The reading half had been settled in this repository for a week.** The checkpoint's `yearless-claim` row already retained `MMdd` admitting `0229` and `ddMM` admitting `2902`. A day-first reading of `MMdd` must refuse `0229` for naming month 29; a month-first reading of `ddMM` must refuse `2902` for the same reason. The admissions are the discriminator, and they were sitting in the retained bytes the whole time.

They were invisible because that row exists to answer *how far does the yearless day bound reach*, and it records the same observations under that heading. February 29 is there as a leap-day boundary, not as an impossible month. A search for "component order", "MMdd order", or "which pair leads" finds nothing, because nothing was filed under those words — the evidence is indexed by the question that motivated the probe, and a later question with a different vocabulary does not reach it. The repository's own rule to grep `spec/` and `docs/sources/` before writing a negative or exclusivity claim was widened the same morning and still did not fire here, because this was not phrased as a negative claim at all. It was phrased as an assumption, which reads like a decision rather than a question.

Treatment. Before recording an assumption, look for the **observations** that would settle it rather than for the words that describe it: list the fixtures and inputs a settling measurement would need, then grep the retained checkpoints for those literal values — a date, a code, a stored text. `0229` finds the row that "component order" cannot. And when a probe is designed, ask what *else* its rows discriminate: this one's admissions answered two questions and were filed under one, which cost a round trip and a shipped assumption that was never true.

## LF122 — an exclusion's stated reason is a claim, and a reason no document could contradict is the gap restated

> Date: 2026-08-31. Sections: §11. Basis: [`SPEC-2026-08-31-08`](A12-DMKITS-SPEC-SYNC-LEDGER.md#spec-2026-08-31-08), accepted at a12-dmkits `7ace4229c`, and the parallel carrier's correction in this repository.

The over-limit clearing correction reached every repeatable-target carrier but one. The indexed-parallel route was excluded, honestly and in writing, and the exclusion gave a reason: a parallel target's index column carries no entry at an over-limit row, so the row may be outside the route's *execution domain* rather than capacity-excluded. That reason was recorded in the code, in the gap, and in the outbound ledger entry, and it was wrong.

**The tell was available without any measurement: the reason and the thing it was meant to explain agreed on every document the estate could produce.** An excess row with no resolvable partner is skipped under the execution-domain account and cleared-with-nothing-to-clear under the capacity account, and those are the same observable. A reason that no available document could contradict is not a reason for the exclusion; it is the exclusion's gap, restated in mechanism vocabulary and thereby made to look settled. Writing it down as a `limit` row made it *more* convincing rather than less, because a stated limit reads as a boundary someone examined.

The peer built the one document that separates them — an excess row with a **resolvable** join — and the row appears in the outcome list, cleared, with the value it could have computed not written. Capacity, not domain. Its third document is what makes `cleared` mean anything at all: an *in-capacity* row whose join resolves to nothing computes the kind's empty value instead of clearing, so the carrier distinguishes *no value to compute* from *this row is not mine to write*.

The correction also falsified a registered law. `parallelNumericTargetRouteClearing_noMarks` said clean index columns clear nothing regardless of target row topology; an over-limit row clears with every column clean. It was restated with the in-capacity hypothesis rather than deleted, and the hypothesis is the part that was previously invisible — the earlier proof closed by `rfl` precisely because the implementation short-circuited before ever looking at the rows.

Treatment. When excluding a carrier from a correction, write the exclusion's reason as a claim and ask which document would distinguish it from the alternative. If none in reach does, say `no witness known` and stop there — do not name a mechanism. Then check whether any law about that carrier closes by `rfl` through a short circuit the correction would remove: that `rfl` is where the unexamined assumption is compiled in. See [`LF116`](#lf116--reusing-a-mechanism-inherits-its-evidences-scope-not-the-mechanisms-generality) for the same hazard in the opposite direction, and [`LF119`](#lf119--a-conditional-failure-law-with-no-witness-is-a-hypothesis-about-the-code-not-a-property-of-it) for the witness rule the exclusion should have met.

## LF123 — an unreachable discriminator is a claim about the quantity you chose, and a silent operator needs a rule for each answer

> Date: 2026-08-31. Sections: §2 and §13. Basis: the [group-operand extent checkpoint](sources/group-and-iteration-probes.md#src-group-operand-over-limit-extent) and the row it replaced in [SG13](SEMANTICS-GAPS.md#sg13--group-list-and-group-count-completion).

A row in the gap ledger closed a discriminator as **unreachable**: separating an all-instantiated row domain from an in-capacity one needs a document whose every row index exceeds capacity, and rows are a dense array from index 1, so no such document exists. The reasoning was sound and the conclusion was wrong. It is a proof about a count of **rows**. The same two domains differ on a count of **cells** the moment one filled cell sits beyond capacity while its in-capacity siblings are instantiated and empty — a document the format allows and the estate could have built at any time.

**An unreachability claim is only ever about the quantity the claimant happened to pick.** It reads like a fact about the document format because the format appears in its reasoning, and the format is genuinely a constraint; what the claim actually establishes is that *this* projection cannot see the difference. Before recording one, name the projection explicitly and ask which other projection over the same state would separate the accounts — here, going from "how many rows" to "how many cells" was the whole distance. That is the same discipline [`LF121`](#lf121--retained-evidence-is-indexed-by-the-question-that-motivated-it-so-the-settler-for-a-new-question-hides-under-an-old-rows-label) applies to retained evidence, one level earlier: there the settler hides under another question's label, here it hides under another quantity's.

**The second half cost a whole round.** The first attempt authored `NumberOfFilledFields(Shell) > 0` and observed it not firing on the over-limit document. That is not a measurement of anything. A validation rule reports only its firing, and `unknown` and `zero` are both silent, so a positive-only rule cannot distinguish "the cell is outside the extent" from "the cell is retained and poisons the count" — which are exactly the two accounts in play, and the estate's own answer was the second one. Adding `NumberOfFilledFields(Shell) < 1` to the same document turned the silence into a fired message and settled it in one run.

Generalize it: **when the observation channel reports only one of an operator's outcomes, a measurement plan needs a rule per answer, not a rule per document.** Every three-valued leaf read through a fired-message channel has this shape. The corollary is a review question with teeth — for any retained row whose evidence is a rule that did *not* fire, ask which other answers would also not have fired; if more than one would, the row proves nothing and should never have been retained.

Treatment. Write a discriminator's projection down beside the claim that it is unreachable, so the next reader can attack the projection rather than re-deriving the format argument. When authoring a probe over a silent operator, author the negative partner in the same request — it costs one rule and it is the difference between a suggestive silence and a measurement. See [`LF118`](#lf118--an-expected-value-derived-from-the-implementation-is-not-a-lock-and-native_decide-makes-it-look-like-one) for the sibling failure inside Lean, where a passing `native_decide` supplies the same false comfort a non-firing rule does here.

## LF124 — preserving an untested lock to stay "evidence-exact" turns it into the claim you are defending

A peer measurement covered six carriers over an **unstarred** group operand and said so. This estate's own consumers were narrowed to match, and then a choice appeared: the same resolvers also serve a *starred* group operand, which the measurement did not cover. The estate's rule is that an unmeasured consumer keeps the complete view, so the narrowing was restricted to the unstarred form and a branch was added to keep the two apart.

The stated reason for that branch looked careful. It was not. The evidence that the starred form behaved differently was a single conformance case asserting `unknown .overRepetition` — with no checkpoint, no comment, and no oracle behind it. That case was the whole basis of the "boundary", and it had been written from the implementation. So the split was not the cautious reading of the measurement; it was **an untested claim promoted to a design invariant in order to avoid contradicting another untested claim**, and it shipped with a conformance case pinning it and a docstring explaining it.

A probe settled it in one request: the starred operand answers identically on every arm — `0` against a `1` control, `5` against a `12` control, and the malformed cell evaluable one index above capacity and blocking one index below. Three conformance cases across two kinds had to be flipped, all of them green the whole time.

**The tell is asymmetric caution.** Declining to *widen* an account past its measurement is the rule. Declining to *narrow* an existing account because an unmeasured case would flip is not the same act, and it does not inherit the rule's justification: both directions are claims, and the one already written down is not privileged for having been written first. When a boundary's only support is a case with no provenance, the boundary is not a boundary — it is the next thing to measure.

Treatment. Before adding a branch to preserve an existing answer, look at what supports that answer: a checkpoint, or a `native_decide` someone wrote from the code ([`LF118`](#lf118--an-expected-value-derived-from-the-implementation-is-not-a-lock-and-native_decide-makes-it-look-like-one)). If the latter, the cheap move is to measure rather than to encode the split — here it was one probe request against a route already in use, and it cost less than the branch, its docstring, and its case did. And when the measurement lands, expect to delete a boundary case rather than to confirm it; a case that exists only to make an unmeasured split visible has done its job when the split disappears.
